Lens rigidity in 2D: The reconstruction of a Riemann surface from its geodesic lengths
Spyros Alexakis, Matti Lassas

Abstract
We address the question of whether a Riemannian manifold-with-boundary ( M , g ) (M,g) in dimension two is uniquely determined from knowledge of the distances between points on its boundary. An affirmative answer is called boundary rigidity for ( M , g ) (M,g) ; it is closely related to lens rigidity. The latter question originates in the problem of reconstructing the speed of sound in an unknown medium from measurements of the travel time of sound waves that are sent in and ultimately return to the boundary. We prove essentially optimal results on these rigidity questions: Our first result answers proves rigidity locally, near a convex portion of the boundary. Our second result proves rigidity globally, for manifolds with convex boundary, in the absence of trapping (closed geodesics), thus confirming a conjecture of Uhlmann. Our final result proves the optimal reconstruction for convex boundaries ∂ M \partial M even in the presence of trapping, showing rigidity up to outermost trapped geodesics. Our results thus extend the classical work of Pestov and Uhlmann [ 58 ] on rigidity of simple 2-manifolds, as well as the many prior results on injectivity of the X-ray transform, which address linearized versions of the rigidity problem. Our method is to treat the (non-linear) rigidity problem directly, where we simultaneously re-cast the lens data as generalized Riemannian circles, and obtain rigidity for these “pseudo-circles”, by studying a system of equations that we show these objects must satisfy. The rigidity we obtain ultimately is proven via novel estimates that are reminiscent of energy-type estimates for hyperbolic equations.



1
Introduction

Let
(M,g)
be a
C^{m}
-smooth,
{m\geq 5}

2-dimensional Riemannian manifold with boundary
\partial M
.
We recall the classical
length map
and
scattering map
associated to the boundary of
(M,g)
.

We denote by

\gamma_{x,v}(t)
the arc-length parametrized geodesic of
(M,g)
for which
\gamma_{x,v}(0)=x\in\overline{M}
and
\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\gamma_{x,v}(t)=v
.
We also denote
\dot{\gamma}_{x,v}(t)=\partial_{t}\gamma_{x,v}(t)
.

Let
TM
and
SM
be the tangent bundle and the unit circle bundle of
M
, respectively.
At
x\in\partial M
let
{\bf n}(x)
denote the unit interior-pointing vector.
We denote

{\partial_{in}SM}=\{(x,v)\in SM:\ x\in\partial M,\langle\xi,{\bf n}\rangle>0\}
and
{\partial_{out}S}M|_{\partial M}=\{(x,v)\in TM:\ x\in\partial M,\langle\xi,{\bf n}\rangle<0\}
.
The length map
\ell=\ell_{M,g}:{S_{in}\partial}M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, is then defined via:

\ell_{M,g}(x,v)=\inf\{t>0\ :\ \gamma_{x,v}(t)\in\partial M\}.

(1.0.1)

It is the smallest time
t
for which the geodesic ray
\gamma_{x,v}({t})
,
{t}>0
hits the boundary.
The
scattering map:
{\mathcal{S}}_{M,g}:\{(x,v)\in{\partial_{in}SM}:\ \ell_{M,g}(x,v)<\infty\}\to{\partial_{out}SM}
is defined via:

\mathcal{S}_{M,g}(x,v)=(\,\gamma_{x,v}(t)\big|_{t=\ell(x,v)},\dot{\gamma}_{x,v}(t)\big|_{t=\ell(x,v)}\,).

(1.0.2)

In other words it measures the point and the tangent to the geodesic
\gamma_{x,v}
when it hits the boundary again.
We call the pair
(\ell_{M,g},\mathcal{S}_{M,g})
the lens data of the manifold
(M,g)
.
As discussed below, see
[
61
]
, on simple manifolds, the lens data is equivalent to the
boundary distance function
–the latter is the function

d_{g}:\partial M\times\partial M\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by the formula

d_{g}(P,Q)={\rm dist}_{\overline{M},g}(P,Q),

where
{\rm dist}_{\overline{M},g}(P,Q)
is simply the distance between the two points
P,Q
in
(\overline{M},g)
.

A classical question in geometric inverse problems is whether knowledge of the lens data uniquely determines the metric
g
.
This is the
question we address in this paper. The question can be posed both globally
[
13
]

and locally
[
71
]
.
The local question is whether knowledge of
\ell_{M,g}
in an open neighborhood of
(p,{\bf t}(p)){\in\overline{\partial_{in}SM}}
, where
p\in\partial M

and
{\bf t}(p)
is the unit tangent vector of
\partial M
at
p
, uniquely determines
g
near
p
.
Our first result concerns this local question and answers it in the affirmative when
\partial M
is convex at
p
:

Rigidity theorems involve comparing two putative Riemannian
manifolds of dimension 2 with identical lens data (i.e., identical scattering maps and length maps), and proving that the two manifolds are isometric. To make this notion precise, we parametrize

{\partial_{in}SM}
: Letting
p\in\partial M
and
{\bf t}(p)
the unit tangent vector field on
\partial M
, and
{\bf n}(p)
the inward-pointing unit normal vector field, the space
\partial_{in}SM
is captured as:

(p,\cos\varphi\cdot{\bf t}(p)+\sin\varphi\cdot{\bf n}{(p)}),p\in\partial M,\varphi\in(0,\pi)
.

Then given two manifolds
M_{1},M_{2}
, any local smooth function

\psi:{\cal U}_{1}\to{\cal U}_{2},{\cal U}_{1}\subset\partial M_{1},{\cal U}_{2}\subset\partial M_{2}
lifts to a map
\psi\oplus Id:{\cal U}_{1}\times(0,\pi)\to{\cal U}_{2}\times(0,\pi)
in the trivial way:
(\psi\oplus Id)(x,\varphi)=(\psi(x),\varphi)
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
be compact,
{\cal C}^{m}
-smooth 2-manifolds with
boundaries
\partial M_{1},\partial M_{2}
.

Let
p_{1}\in\partial M_{1}
be a convex point for
\partial M_{1}
. Assume there is an open neighborhood
{\cal U}_{1}
of
p_{{1}}\in{\partial M_{1}}
and a local diffeorphism
\psi:{\cal U}_{1}\to{\cal U}_{2}\subset{\partial M_{2}}
so that:

\displaystyle\ell_{M_{1},g_{1}}=\ell_{M_{2},g_{2}}\circ(\psi\oplus Id)_{*},

(1.0.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{M_{2},g_{2}}=\mathcal{S}_{M_{1},g_{1}}\circ(\psi\oplus Id)_{*}

on
{\cal U}_{1}\times\bigg((0,\epsilon){\bigcup}(\pi-\epsilon,\pi)\bigg)
, for some
\epsilon>0
.

Then the points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}=\psi(p_{1})

have neighborhoods
\Omega_{1}\subset M_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}\subset M_{2}
, respectively, such that

(\Omega_{1},g_{1}|_{\Omega_{1}})
and
(\Omega_{2},g_{2}|_{\Omega_{2}})
are isometric.

Remark 1.1
.

We note that near a convex point of any Riemann manifold, knowledge of the boundary distance function
{\rm dist}_{\partial M\times\partial M}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\partial M
close to a point
p\in\partial M
where
\partial M
is convex
determines first the intrinsic metric
g|_{\partial M}
of the boundary
\partial M
and also
the lens map

(\ell_{(M,g)},{\cal S}_{(M,g)})
in open neighborhoods of

p\times\{0\},p\times\{\pi\}
, see Lemma 1.2 in
[
61
]
.

Thus the above theorem also implies local boundary rigidity,
in the sense that on any two-dimensional
{\cal C}^{m}
-smooth Riemannian manifold with boundary, and any convex point
p\in\partial M
, knowing
the distance function
{\rm dist}_{\partial M\times\partial M}(x,y)
for
x,y
near
p

determines the metric
g
inside
M
, near
p
.

Theorem
1
implies the following global reconstruction result for non-trapping manifolds,
which solves Uhlmann’s scattering rigidity conjecture, see

[
74
]
, for Riemannian surfaces with convex boundary.
When
\ell(x,v)<\infty
of all
(x,v)\in SM
, we say that
(M,g)
is non-trapping. (For 2-manifolds with
strictly
convex boundary, this is equivalent to the
non-existence of closed geodesics in the interior of
M
).

Theorem 2
.

Let
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
be compact, oriented, smooth non-trapping manifolds
with strictly convex boundary. Moreover, assume that
\psi:\partial M_{1}\to\partial M_{2}

is a diffeomorphism such that on
\partial M_{1}{\times}(0,\pi)
:

\ell_{M_{1},g_{1}}=\ell_{M_{2},g_{2}}\circ{(\psi\oplus Id)_{*}},\quad\mathcal{S}_{M_{2},g_{2}}=\mathcal{S}_{M_{1},g_{1}}\circ{(\psi\oplus Id)_{*}}.

(1.0.4)

Then
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
are isometric Riemannian manifolds.

We note that non-trapping manifolds with a convex boundary may have geodesics with conjugate points. In particular the above theorem applies beyond the setting of simple manifolds, which was resolved by Pestov and Uhlmann in
[
58
]
.

For trapping manifolds, a part of the unknown manifold can be reconstructed:

Theorem 3
.

Let
(M_{1},g_{1})
and
(M_{2},g_{2})
be compact, oriented, smooth manifolds
with strictly convex boundary and
\psi:\partial M_{1}\to\partial M_{2}

be a diffeomorphism such that (
1.0.4
) is valid. Let
\partial M_{1}^{k},\partial M_{2}^{k}
,
k=1\dots,K
be the connected components of these boundaries.
Then for any
k\in\{1,\dots,K\}
both boundaries
\partial M^{{k}}_{j}
, where
j=1,2
, have neighborhoods
W^{{k}}_{j}\subset M^{{k}}_{j}

such that
\partial W^{{k}}_{j}
are outermost
closed geodesics
\gamma_{j}
of
M_{j}
with respect to
\partial M_{j}^{k}
and the map
\psi:\partial M_{1}\to\partial M_{2}
extends
to a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\psi}:W_{1}\to W_{2}
and
g_{1}|_{W_{1}}=\tilde{\psi}^{*}g_{2}|_{W_{2}}
.

(The requirement that
\partial W_{j}=\gamma_{j}
to be “outermost” closed geodesics we mean that any other closed geodesic in
M_{j}
is contained inside
M_{j}\setminus W_{j}
).

One can show (by slight modification of Croke’s counterexamples for lens and boundary rigidity for trapping manifolds),

[
15
]
, that
Theorem
3
is an optimal result for trapping manifolds having dimension 2
and a convex boundary.

We note that the local result can be manifestly applied to manifolds
where a portion of the boundary is convex, but not the entire boundary.
Clearly, one can obtain the rigidity of the metric near the convex portion of the boundary. The precise optimal result that can be obtained in this setting is left to a future paper.

1.1
Broader Context.

The question of lens rigidity and boundary rigidity have received much attention over the years. They are partly motivated by the importance of this question in geophysics and tomography:

Travel time tomography deals with the problem of determining the sound speed
(or index of refraction) of a medium by measuring the arrival times of waves traveling
through the medium
[
20
,
33
,
34
,
46
,
61
,
72
]
,
starting from the work of Herglotz
[
31
]
. This inverse problem is encountered in geophysics where
observations of the sound or seismic waves on the surface of the Earth is used to determine the substructure of the Earth
and in medical imaging problems and sonography, see references in the reviews
[
10
,
49
]
.
The wave speed of waves in the medium is often modeled by a Riemannian metric

g=g_{ij}(x)dx^{i}dx^{j}

for which the Riemannian distance
d_{g}(x,y)
is the travel time of the waves from
the point
x
to
y
.
Many natural materials are anisotropic, that is, the corresponding to a
Riemannian metric that is not conformally Euclidean.

In the travel time tomography problem, one knows only the travel times and the exit directions of the waves that are sent from the boundary: If one considers a wave packet sent from a boundary point to a given direction, these data correspond to knowing
the travel times and the starting and the exit directions of the geodesic starting at the boundary points. Thus, travel time tomography reduces to the lens rigidity problem we study here.

Earlier Results

As a problem purely in geometric inverse problems, lens rigidity can be studied both
locally
and
globally
. A further key distinction is between dimensions
n=2
and
n\geq 3
.

Prior to discussing the earlier results,
let us observe that from a formal dimension-counting,
the lens rigidity problem in dimensions
n\geq 3
is
over-determined
:
The space of unknowns (Riemannian metrics) is (after gauge normalization) locally captured by

{n\choose{2}}

functions of
n
variables, while the space of data (the space of
lens maps) consists of a function over
\partial M\times\partial M
, hence a
function of
2n-2
variables. In this sense, in dimensions
n>2

(where
2n-2>n
), the
problem of reconstructing the metric from the lens data
is an
over
-determined problem, while for
n=2
the problem is
formally determined.

A class of manifolds that have historically been more
studied in geometric inverse problems are
simple
manifolds:
A manifold
(M,g)
is simple if its boundary is strictly convex,
any two points are joined by a unique minimizing geodesic, which has no conjugate points.

The problem of determining
a simple manifold from lens data is an open problem and Michel conjectured
[
45
]

that every simple compact Riemannian manifold
with boundary is boundary rigid.

The known results are quite different in the dimension
n\geq 3
, where the inverse problem is
formally overdetermined, and in the dimension
n=2
when the problem is formally determined, so we consider
these results separately.
Up to the present paper, results in dimensions
n\geq 3
are
more plentiful.
After the earlier works

[
16
,
13
,
25
,
47
,
52
,
58
,
60
,
68
,
70
]
,
Stefanov, Uhlmann, and Vasy made a breakthrough
[
71
]
by showing that
n
-manifolds (
n\geq 3
) which admit a
convex foliation by smooth 1-parameter family of codimension 1 submanifolds
are lens rigid.
In particular,
[
71
]
proves the boundary
lens rigidity for simply connected compact manifolds of dimension
n\geq 3
which have
strictly convex boundaries and in addition
\partial M
in
M
has no focal points, or
(M,g)
has negative sectional curvature.

For
n=2
, a
seminal result was obtained by Pestov and Uhlmann
[
58
]
, for the global problem on topological discs, under the assumption that the boundary is convex and the metric inside is simple. In the remarkable proof

[
58
]

to the boundary rigidity problem for 2-dimensional simple manifolds, the problem was reduced to Calderon’s problem for Laplace-Beltrami operator
of determining the class of the metric of the manifold from the Cauchy data of harmonic functions.
Calderon’s inverse problem in the 2-dimensional case was solved (for smooth conductivities) by Nachman
[
48
]

and finally for bounded coefficients by Astala and Päivärinta
[
4
]
; Nachman’s proof appeared around a decade
after the problem in dimensions
n\geq 3
was proven to be uniquely solvable by Sylvester and
Uhlmann
[
73
]
. The hardness of the Calderon problem in 2D compared to higher dimensions can again be morally seen in that the former is formally determined whereas the latter formally over-determined.

Remarkable as the proof of Pestov and Uhlmann is, it is restricted to a
global problem
(one needs to know the length data for the entire boundary and reconstructs the entire manifold). It also relies crucially on the assumption of simplicity. It also is worth noting that a transformation of this proof to a reconstruction algorithm will necessarily suffer from the severe instability associated to the Calderon problem, see
[
37
,
43
]
. It is very unclear whether this instability is inherent, or a feature of their method. Beyond questions of stability,
still for the 2D global problem, the assumption of simplicity and of convex
boundary is an assumption one would of course like to dispose of.
In fact, the manifolds that are
not
simple present mathematical interest, and are also important in practice: The presence of conjugate points arises due to strong lensing effects. This leads to a complicated structure in the measured data set, caused by caustics (i.e., focusing of geodesics) and by the multi-pathing, that is, the existence of several geodesics between the
sources and observation points. In practice, conjugate points are commonly present in seismic
imaging. For this reason they are used in standard models for testing algorithms, such as in the widely used Marmousi model
[
8
]
.

The second setting of interest where simplicity fails is the presence of trapped geodesics. In such a setting the structure of lens data can be drastically affected. For example one can have geodesics emanating from a point on the boundary
\partial M
that never return to
\partial M
.
This leads to limitations in the information contained in the measured lens data.

In fact it is known that
lens rigidity does
not
hold unconditionally, as seen in examples by Croke
[
15
,
18
]
, which show that one cannot obtain rigidity past the outermost
trapped geodesic. We also note that in a certain sense one cannot expect rigidity for concave boundaries: Boundary rigidity is clearly false, since the minimal length geodesics of
\overline{M}
stay on the boundary entirely, and thus provide no information on the metric in
{\rm int}(M)
. Lens rigidity
for manifolds with concave boundary can be formulated, but one can easily
construct examples with a trapped geodesic arbitrarily close to the concave
boundary, and then adapt the Croke counterexamples to see that lens rigidity
fails. Thus the global lens rigidity makes sense only in the context of an (at least partly)
convex boundary.

In this connection Uhlmann conjectured in
[
74
]
that for manifolds with convex boundary, trapping is the only obstruction to rigidity: That manifolds with no trapped geodesics are lens rigid.
1
1

1

The assumption that the scattering map in Conjecture 4.1 in
[
74
]
is well-defined is equivalent to the absence of trapping.
Our Theorem
2
above
proves this conjecture for Riemannian 2-manifolds.

Beyond these global rigidity results, there are many instances in practice where one does not have access to
the
entire
lens data on the boundary
\partial M
, but rather just to data that are localized at a
portion
of the boundary. In those settings
one would also like a
partial data
(or
local)
result: Given lens data suitably
localized near a point
P
on the boundary, reconstruct the metric near that point
P
. (Note this question only makes sense when
\partial M
is convex at
P
). Our Theorem
1
provides precisely such a result (using localized data both near a point
P\in\partial M
and localized in directions
to lie near the tangent vector
\vec{t}(P)
of
\partial M
at
P
) under the
necessary assumption that
\partial M
is convex at
P
.

However, beyond this local reconstruction and beyond the resolution
to Uhlmann’s conjecture, one would like results without assuming
convexity of the entire boundary. The local Theorem
1

proves that rigidity holds in open neighborhoods of the convex portion of the
boundary. What the optimal result one can extract by repeatedly applying Theorem
1
in this more general context is left to a future paper.

Next, we discuss the prior approaches to lens and boundary rigidity grouped by the methods they employ. A good reference on this and related geometric inverse problems is the monograph
[
55
]
.
Several results on the boundary rigidity problem and
the inverse travel time problem are based on microlocal analysis
of the geodesic X-ray transforms which arise in the study
of the linearized inverse travel time problem, see
[
12
,
21
,
32
,
46
,
53
,
56
,
54
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
66
,
75
]
and the results for non-simple manifolds,
[
66
]
. These microlocal techniques have been applied for the uniqueness of the boundary rigidity problem, e.g., in
[
63
,
68
]
and for stability results for the
of the boundary rigidity problem,
e.g., in
[
5
,
64
,
65
]
.
For manifolds of dimension
n\geq 3
microlocal techniques has
been applied also for the lens and scattering rigidity problems
for simple manifolds
[
78
]

and with trapping of hyperbolic type in
[
15
,
27
,
30
]
.

Lens rigidity has been also proven for more special classes of manifolds, for example those having constant curvature
or product structure in
[
14
,
18
]
. Perturbative results on
lens rigidity, that is, rigidity
for perturbations of lens rigid manifolds has been studied in
[
17
,
67
]
.
A very different, purely geometric approach to the study of global boundary rigidity problem has
been successfully deployed to perturbative results around domains in Euclidean and hyperbolic space, see
[
9
,
35
]
; they do this by connecting the problem to minimal surface problems, or it generalization, minimal volume problems,
In these studies the manifold is embedded into a function space using distance functions and the uniqueness is established by studying minimal surfaces close to affine planes. This approach makes a strong use of the simplicity assumption and thus it cannot be used for local reconstructions, which is the main result here (the global rigidity results follow from the local one).
Other approaches to the problem
has used the lengths of geodesics to determine the
C^{\infty}
-jet of the Riemannian metric in boundary
normal coordinates, see
[
40
,
76
,
80
]
; one can obtain results under the extra assumption that the metric be real-analytic,
see
[
26
,
77
]
.

Lens and boundary rigidity (which are by their very nature problems for manifolds with boundary) have interesting analogues for closed manifolds.
The analog of the lens rigidity problem for closed manifolds is the inverse problem for the marked length spectrum.
In this problem, a manifold needs to be reconstructed form the lengths of the closed geodesic, indexed by
the homotopy class of the geodesic, see
[
11
,
29
,
28
,
51
]

on the progress on this problem for negatively curved and Anosov manifolds.

Boundary rigidity has also deep connections
to physical problems. These include inverse problems for manifolds with magnetic geodesics

[
3
,
19
,
79
]
,
Yang-Mills fields
[
57
]
,
inverse source problems
[
20
,
39
]
, elasticity
[
22
,
69
]

and space-time tomography of Lorentzian manifolds
[
1
,
23
,
41
,
50
,
62
]
.

In the next subsection we outline the new ideas in this work.
To contrast our method with those of some of the results we presented above,
let us note that many of these earlier methods for the lens rigidity problem rely on a two-step approach to solving the inverse problem:
In a first step, one seeks to address the
linearized version
of the problem first.
In the case at hand, the linearized rigidity problem reduces the question to showing the triviality of the
kernel of an X-ray operator on symmetric tensors. Given the geometric nature of the problem, one also seeks to normalize the gauge, to disallow
pure gauge elements in the kernel of this operator.
Having resolved the linearized rigidity problem on an appropriate open set of backgrounds,
[
71
]
proceed by a pseudo-linearization, which allows to upgrade to the true (non-linear) problem, by relying on a surjectivity of the map, having modified the integral equations to add non-linear modification in the kernel of the operator.

In contrast, we do
not
perform any linearization; instead we seek to
tackle the non-linear problem directly, as we explain in the next section.
As we also will discuss there, the reconstruction is based on estimates which resemble those that one derives in hyperbolic equations.
We believe that both these features of our method is an advantage,
and translating the rigidity to a reconstruction algorithm may prove more fruitful with this direct approach.

In this connection, we point out certain known
instability
results: The linearization of the

local
lens data,
(i.e., the Fŕechét derivative in the metric tensor) of the map from the metric tensor to the
lens data (linearizing around the Euclidean metric) agrees (for perturbations of the metric tensor of
small enough support) with the limited angle tensor X-ray transform. It is known that
the inversion problem for
limited-angle X-ray transform is ill-posed in that sense that it cannot enjoy Hölder-type stability, see the instability
results of Koch-Rüland-Salo, Proposition 6.7 and Theorem 6.3 in
[
37
]
, and the earlier results in
Assylbekov-Stefanov
[
2
]
.

We note however that in dimensions
n\geq 3
, in a neighborhood
W\subset M
of a point
p
in the convex part of the boundary
\partial M
, both the inverse problem for the limited angle X-ray transform and the
reconstruction of the conformal factor of the metric from the distance data are Hölder-stable, as show by Stefanov, Uhlmann, and Vasy in
[
65
]
.
The key reason for the stability is that in dimensions
n\geq 3
, for any point
q\in W
and any direction
\xi

there is a geodesic that passes through
q
, is normal to
\xi
, and intersects the boundary in the both directions emanating from
q
.
Whether and how our estimates here can be upgraded to a stable reconstruction is left to a future paper.

1.2
Outline of the ideas.

We outline the main ideas in the proof of the local Theoreom
1
. The presentation here is in very broad strokes. A more detailed explanation of each idea is provided in individual sections below, after some key notions have been properly defined.

As a starting point of our approach, we remark that there are two different notions of identifying a Riemannian metric:
(a) As a metric space, where one seeks to reconstruct the distance function between any two points and (b) expressed in a normalized coordinate system, where one seeks to reconstruct the components of the metric tensor with respect to these coordinates. A key attribute of our method is that we reconstruct our metric according to

both notions
, in a coupled way.

One central challenge is how to identify

points
in the manifold through the
scattering map: To identify a point
A\in M
we seek to identify an (
A
-dependent)
focusing vector field

\big(p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A),{t(\theta;A)\big)\in\partial_{in}SM\times[0,\infty)}
.

The focusing vector field
\theta\to(p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A))\in\partial_{in}SM
,
\theta\in[0,\pi]

is defined by requiring
all the geodesic segments
\gamma_{p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A)}(t)

focus
at the same point
A\in M
, after a
\theta
-dependent distance
t(\theta;A)
is traversed, that is

\gamma_{p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A)}(t(\theta;A))=\gamma_{p(\theta^{\prime};A),v(\theta^{\prime};A)}(t(\theta^{\prime};A))\quad\hbox{for all }\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in[0,\pi]

see the right image in Fig.
1
.

\partial M
A
p(0,A)
p(\theta_{1},A)
p(\theta_{2},A)
p(\theta_{2},A)
p(\theta_{4},A)

\partial M
A
p(0,A)
p(\theta_{1},A)
p(\theta_{2},A)
p(\theta_{2},A)
p(\theta_{4},A)

Figure 1:
LEFT IMAGE: A fan of geodesics (in red) emanating from a point
A
in
M
, in (green) directions
\vec{\nu}(\theta)
all of which intersect the boundary
\partial M
at points
p(\theta,A)
. The “fan” of angles
\theta
has “opening”
\pi
.

RIGHT FIGURE: Dual image of the left. The vector field
\big(p(A;\theta),v(A,\theta)\big)
(in green) along a subinterval in
\partial M
. (The vectors in green are
v(A,\theta)
at
p(\theta;A)
).
Here the “fan”
\theta
has angle
\pi
. The red lines are the geodesics
\gamma_{p(A;\theta),v(A,\theta)}
, which all focus at the point
A
.

This is equivalent to identifying the
exit
points, directions
and the lengths
of geodesics that originate from a fixed point
A
in the manifold, and cover a “fan”
\pi
of vectors based at
A
.
Equivalently, given a
A\in M
, the “focusing vector field”

\big(p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A),{t(\theta;A)\big)}

can be visualized
as the points
S_{out}\partial M\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
where a wave-front generated by a point
A

(visualized as an evolving family of half-circles of expanding radius
emanating from
A
)
cuts

\partial M
. Equivalently, letting

\gamma_{A,\vec{\nu}(\theta)}
be the geodesic emanating from
A
in the direction of a vector
\vec{\nu}(\theta)
based at
A
then
p(\theta;A)
is the point of intersection of
\gamma_{A,\vec{\nu}(\theta)}
with
\partial M
and
v(\theta;A)
is the tangent vector to
\gamma_{A,\vec{\nu}(\theta)}
at
p(\theta)
(see the right picture in Fig.
1
).

However we do
not

aim to identify the “focusing vector fields”,

\big(p(\theta;A),

v(\theta;A),

{t(\theta;A)}\big)
that correspond to each point
A

individually
. Rather, we seek to identify a

family
of focusing vector fields

\big(p(\theta;A),v(\theta;A),{t(\theta;A)}\big)
, where
A
lies in
an open set around the point
P
.
2
2

2

We point out a loose connection here with the resolution of
the Calderon problem in
n=2
for
L^{\infty}
conductivities:
Whereas in dimensions
n\geq 3
and
n=2
with smooth enough
conductivities solutions of Calderon’s problem

[
73
,
48
]
are based on constructing individual complex geometric optics
solutions of the elliptic equations, in the setting

n=2
but only
L^{\infty}
conductivities,
[
4
]
,
the solution
involves constructing a large
family
of complex geometric optics
solutions which are considered simultaneously using degree theory.

We remark that

if
one where somehow able to identify all these “families of focusing vector fields”
then using the knowledge of the lens data one would identify
(M,g)

as a metric space. This follows using the artificial extension of our manifold that we introduce below;
in that setting the “focusing vector fields” can be replaced by
half-circles
that are centered at points inside the
unknown
part of the manifold. Then if we were able to identify a family of such circles (in the artificial extension), whose centers lie in an open neighborhood in the
unknown
part of the manifold, then we can reconstruct
(M,g)
in this open neighborhood
as a metric space
. This follows from classical arguments — see for example
[
20
]
and
[
6
,
36
,
38
]
.

Our approach here is precisely to do this identification; however we cannot
purely
identify this 2-parameter family of half-circles
independently
of finding the metric in a coordinate system: the identification of the family of circles is
done
in conjunction
with identifying the metric in a certain
coordinate system
(suitable Gaussian coordinates
x,y
for the point
A
, coupled with a
suitable polar coordinate

\theta\in[0,\pi]
on each point
A
). In fact the essence of our
work here is to exploit how the families of focusing vector fields
(in fact, a variant of them which we call “circlefronts”) obey first order equations in the parameters
x,y

which are
coupled
to the first and second fundamental forms
of
g

in Gaussian coordinates

(x,y)
with respect to which
g
is of the form

dx^{2}+g_{yy}(x,y)dy^{2}
.

Let us say a few words about the key properties that define
our families of focusing vector fields: There are three key ingredients. A common thread is
that we study the linearization of the geodesic equation (captured by
Jacobi fields), and essentially we impose conditions on the points where the geodesics we are tracking
hit the boundary
\partial M
. The Jacobi fields correspond to variations in the
parameters
x,y,\theta
.

The first ingredient is a certain conservation
law of a symplectic product of any such pairs of Jacobi fields.
The second property is a commutation property of any pair of such
Jacobi fields. The third property
links
one of these Jacobi
fields with the metric component
g_{yy}(x,y)
(in the Gaussian
coordinate system) — more precisely with the derivatives

{\partial_{x}^{j}}g_{yy}(x,y),j\leq 2
. In essence, we show that imposing these
properties uniquely determines the 2-parameter
families of focusing vector fields
\big(p(\theta;x,y),v(\theta;x,y),{t(\theta;x,y)}\big)
.

For convenience, we will not be restricting our analysis to the boundary
\partial M
, but rather considering an artificial extension of our metric to a larger manifold
\widetilde{M}
, divided into a
known
region (the extension
\widetilde{M}\setminus M
) and the
unknown region
(
M
). The family of expanding half-circles (emanating from a point
A\in M
) is called a
circle-front
, and an extra parameter
t
is introduced to capture the “time” coordinate of the expansion (equivalently, the radius of each half-circle, up to sign).
Given the assumed local convexity of the boundary, for
A
close to the boundary, these circle-fronts
exit
the unknown region
M
and subsequently lie in the known region
\widetilde{M}\setminus M
.
The 2-parameter family of circle-fronts is thus captured by a map

\vec{\Psi}:(x,y,\theta,t)\to S^{1}\widetilde{M}
(i.e. from a parameter space which is a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, into the circle bundle over
\widetilde{M}
). In particular, our proof relies not on identifying the “families of focusing vector fields” just sketched, but rather the
equivalent
notion of
circle-front families, which are just the
extension
of the focusing vector fields when we continue the geodesic flow from
p(\theta;x,y)
along
v(\theta,x,y)
, so as to lie in the
artificially constructed extension

\widetilde{M}\setminus M
of our manifold.

In the end, we obtain local lens rigidity theorem (near
any convex point on
\partial M
), which compares two metrics with the
same lens data and shows that their circle-fronts

and, at the same time
their first and second fundamental forms
agree, showing that they are isometric. An aspect of the argument
that we wish to highlight is that we derive that the

differences
of the key parameters (essentially three functions

(a,b,c)
of three variables
(x,y,\theta)
which are
differences
of the pre-images of the two circle-front families, taken over the (common) parameter space and the function

{\delta}\kappa(x,y)
of two variables (which is the difference of the two geodesic curvatures) satisfy a system of 1st order PDEs
coupled with a
transport equation; the solution to the latter transport equations couples to the main parameters
(a,b,c)(x,y,\theta)
and
\delta\kappa(x,y)
via a complicated integral operator over the parameter space
x,y,\theta,t
. Note in particular that the system of unknowns involves both functions of
(x,y,\theta)
and functions of only
(x,y)
.
This system (suitably
analyzed after taking two derivatives) admits an energy-type estimate similar to the type of closed estimates one derives in systems of wave and transport equations. The local equality of lens data
translates into zero initial data for this system, from which rigidity
follows. It is worth noting that taking two derivatives of our system complicates the analysis, yet is necessary since in a key way we rely on a Sobolev trace theorem to link the functions
\delta\alpha(x,y),\delta\kappa(x,y)
of two variables to the
restriction
of
\partial_{\theta}(a,b,c)
to the hypersurface
\{\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}\}
.

We hope that the methods presented here lend themselves
to not just a study of rigidity of lens data, but hopefully to a
suitable notion of
stability
of the reconstruction of
g

from lens data. Importantly, since our argument is based on a regular energy-type argument rather than a Carleman-type estimate, one might expect
that the stability afforded by the analysis developed herein will be more robust than that typically implied by analysis based on Carleman estimates.
This challenge is left to the future.

2
Circlefront families, pseudo-Circlefront families, and reduction of
lens rigidity to their uniqueness.

We now introduce in detail the main construction of objects on which our work will rely (Circle-fronts and pseudo-circlefronts). These notions are novel, and lie at the heart of our approach to lens rigidity — they are defined out of the (local)
lens data of a manifold; our approach is to impose a rich enough class of properties on these objects so that two manifolds with the
same lens data must ultimately have the same circlefronts and
(as a consequence we will obtain) be
isometric.
These notions rely on a technical step of constructing an artificial extension of our manifold, and defining an extended scattering map on this enlarged manifold. We commence with this
construction.

2.1
The artificial extension of
(M,g)
, and the extended scattering map

Consider two manifolds
(M_{j},g_{j})
as in Theorem
1
.
Let
{\bf n}_{j}:\partial M_{j}\to TM_{j}
denote the unit normal vector of
\partial M_{j}
, pointing into
M_{j}
. Let
\Sigma_{j}\subset\partial M_{j}

be a strictly convex subset of the boundary (i.e. its geodesic curvature with respect to
{\bf n}_{j}
is strictly negative) such that
there
\psi(\Sigma_{1})=\Sigma_{2}.

Then, by
[
40
]
(see also

[
66
,
80
]
), the

-jets of the metric tensors (in the boundary normal coordinates)
of

and
(M_{2},g_{2})
coincide on
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively.

By using the
C^{{m}}
-jet of metric on the boundary, we can construct extended
manifold
(\widetilde{M}_{j},\widetilde{g}_{j})
which contain the original manifolds
(M_{j},g_{j})
as isometric subsets,
by extending

3
3

3

We should write

the manifold smoothly across

near the chosen point

The extension of metrics across

Crucially, given that the jet of the metrics on

For the two manifolds

.

Definition 2.1.1

S_{j}^{t}=S_{{\widetilde{M}_{j},M_{j}}}^{t}:S(\widetilde{M}_{j}\setminus M_{j}^{int})\to S(\widetilde{M}_{j}\setminus M_{j}^{int})\cup\{\emptyset\},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}

\phi(x,y)=\gamma_{\beta(y),\nu(y)}(x).

(2.2.2)

We choose

smaller if needed

In the coordinates

g=dx^{2}+g_{yy}(x,y)dy^{2}.

(2.2.3)

From this point onwards, these coordinates
(x,y)

are the coordinates with respect to which the regularity of all objects of study will be measured. As is classically known, expressing a

metric in Gaussian coordinates means that
g_{yy}
and
\partial_{x}g_{yy}
,
\partial^{2}_{x}g_{yy}

are
{\cal C}^{m-2}
functions.
The geodesic curvature of the curves

with respect to
\partial_{x}\phi(x,y)
) is denoted by

\kappa(x,y)=\frac{1}{g_{yy}(x,y)}\partial_{x}(g_{yy}(x,y)).

(2.2.4)

Remark 2.1
.

Since (as noted above) the lens data near

\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}

Remark 2.2
.

For future reference

2.2.2

\langle\partial_{x},\xi(x,y,\theta)\rangle=\sin(\theta),\quad\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\partial_{y},\xi(x,y,\theta)\rangle=\cos(\theta).

On the other hand, by the definition of geodesic curvature,

\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\nabla_{\partial_{y}}\partial_{x}\,,\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\partial_{y}\rangle=\kappa(x,y)

and

\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\nabla_{\partial_{y}}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\partial_{y})\,,\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{g_{yy}(x,y)}}\partial_{y}\rangle=0.

(\partial_{y},\partial_{x})
define a right-handed basis, and the unit vector
\vec{\xi}(x,y,\theta)|_{\theta=0}\in TM

is a positive multiple of
\partial_{y}
.
In terms of the polar coordinate
\theta
introduced in the tangent space,
R(\theta)=\theta-\frac{\pi}{2}
.

We point out that the parametrization of directions satisfy

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\xi(x,y,\theta)=-R\xi(x,y,\theta),

(2.2.16)

We will next introduce the key notions of “circlefronts” and then “circlefront
families”. These definitions apply in the setting of a manifold
(M,g)
and a local
Gaussian polar coordinate chart

(x,y,\theta)\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]\times[-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}]\times[0,\pi]
.
Given any fixed
(x_{*},y_{*}){\in\Omega_{small}}
the corresponding circlefront is the collection of all half-circles emanating from
\phi(x_{*},y_{*})
in the directions

{\xi(x_{*},y_{*},}\theta)\in[0,\pi]
, for negative values of
t
. The circlefront family is the union of all these circlefronts, over
(x,y)\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]\times[-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}]
. More
formally:

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let

E_{t}:S\widetilde{M}\to S\widetilde{M}
be the geodesic flow operation
on
(M,g)
(see (
2.1.3
)).
The Circle front family associated to a manifold
(M,g)
and a Gaussian polar coordinate chart
(\phi,\xi){:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\owns(x,y,\theta)\to S^{1}\widetilde{M}}

is
a map
\vec{\Psi}_{0}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]\to S\widetilde{M}
defined by

\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t){=(\gamma_{\phi(x,y),\xi(x,y,\theta)}(t),\dot{\gamma}_{\phi(x,y),\xi(x,y,\theta)}(t))}.

(2.2.17)

Denote further

\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)=(\,\phi(x,y),\xi(x,y,\theta)\,)\in S\widetilde{M}.

(2.2.18)

We also denote
\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)=(\Psi_{0}^{1{}}(x,y,\theta,t),\Psi_{0}^{2{}}(x,y,\theta,t))\in S\widetilde{M},

that is,

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)=\gamma_{\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)}(t),\quad\Psi_{0}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)=\dot{\gamma}_{\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)}(t).

For any
0<\epsilon_{2}<\epsilon_{0}
and
0<\delta_{2}<\delta_{0}
, letting

\Omega^{\prime}=[-\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{2}]\times[-\delta_{2},\delta_{2}]\times[0,\pi]
we say that

\vec{\Psi}:\Omega^{\prime}\times[{-2T},{0}]\to S\widetilde{M}
is a Circle front family in
\Omega^{\prime}
when the above conditions are valid for
(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega^{\prime}
.

\theta_{1}
\theta_{2}
\vec{\xi}(x,y,\theta_{2})
\vec{\xi}(x,y,\theta_{1})
\vec{\xi}(x,y,0)
(x,y)
\partial M
\widetilde{M}\setminus M
.
M
\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta_{2},-T)

\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,0,-T)
\bigcup_{\theta\in[0,\pi]}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,-T)

:

\vec{\xi}(x,y,\theta)

(x,y)

.

A
circlefront
is the restriction of a circlefront family to a fixed
x=x_{*},y=y_{*}
. A further restriction of a circlefront to a fixed
t=t_{*}
defines a
circle
. (See Fig.
4
for a depiction of a circle at time
t=-T
). On the other hand restricting a circlefront family to a fixed
t=t_{*}
defines a
circle family
.

5

5

Very strictly speaking, we
should use the term “half-circle”, “half-circle family” etc. We avoid this for languade simplicity.

Remark 2.4
.

Observe that by construction, the regularity of the circle-fronts
(viewed as maps from
\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]
to
S(\widetilde{M}
)
is
{\cal C}^{m-1}
in
t
and
{\cal C}^{m-2}
in
x,y,\theta
. This is because the flow in
t
is described by the geodesic equation while in the other three variables it is captured by a Jacobi equation; the lower-order
coefficients of the former involve Christoffel symbols while of the latter they involve the Gauss curvature.

The circlefront families are particular instances of more general maps which we call
pseudo
-circlefront families that will be important objects in our paper; we introduce these in the next subsection, after introducing some notions we will need.

We consider

\hbox{Param}=\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}

(2.2.19)

as a parameter space and consider any smooth non-degenerate map

\vec{\Psi}:\hbox{Param}\to S^{1}\widetilde{M}.

In particular for every
(x,y,\theta,t)\in{\rm Param}

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)=(\Psi^{1},\vec{\Psi}^{2})(x,y,\theta,t)
;
where
\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)\in M
and

\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\in S^{1}M|_{\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)}
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

We say that a
{\cal C}^{m-1}

map
(x,y,\theta,t)\to\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)\in S\widetilde{M}
is adapted to the geodesic flow
of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
if

E_{t}(\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t_{1}))=\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t_{1}+t)

(2.2.20)

for all
t,t+t_{1}\in[{-2T},{0}]
.

For the rest of this paper, any
\vec{\Psi}
will everywhere stand for a
map adapted to the geodesic flow.

Recall that a vector field
\vec{Z}:\mathbb{R}\to T\widetilde{M}
is a Jacobi field
along the geodesic
\gamma(t)
if
\vec{Z}\in T_{\gamma(t)}\widetilde{M}
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}(t)}^{2}\vec{Z}(t)+R_{\widetilde{M}}(\vec{Z}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))\dot{\gamma}(t)=0

(2.2.21)

where
R_{\widetilde{M}}
is the curvature tensor of
{\widetilde{M}}
.

Definition 2.2.3
.

Given
any map
\vec{\Psi}:{\rm Param}\to S^{1}\widetilde{M}
adapted to the geodesic flow, then along each geodesic

t\to\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)
we define the Jacobi fields

\displaystyle\vec{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t),

(2.2.22)

\displaystyle\vec{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t),

(2.2.23)

\displaystyle\vec{X}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t).

(2.2.24)

Given the equation (
2.2.21
) and the above definition, we see that a map
\vec{\Psi}
as in Definition
2.2.2
can at best by
{\cal C}^{m-1}
in all parameters
(x,y,\theta,t)
. We will be imposing that regularity on all maps
\vec{\Psi}
in this paper.

Definition 2.2.4
.

Consider the
{\cal C}^{m-1}
-smooth map

\Psi:{\rm Param}\to S^{1}\tilde{M}
. For the field
\vec{Y}
, we define the normal and parallel components

\displaystyle Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=g(\vec{Y}(x,y,\theta,t),R\Psi^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)),

(2.2.25)

\displaystyle Y^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=g(\vec{Y}(x,y,\theta,t),\Psi^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)).

Their time-derivatives are denoted by

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{t}Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),\ \ \dot{Y}^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{t}Y^{//}(x,y,\theta,t).

(2.2.26)

We use analogous notations
{\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp},{\bf B}^{//},\dot{\bf B}^{//}
for the Jacobi field
\vec{\bf B}

and
X^{\perp},\dot{X}^{\perp},

X^{//},\dot{X}^{//}
for the Jacobi field
\vec{X}
.

For
C^{m-1}
-smooth maps
\Psi:{\rm Param}\to S^{1}\tilde{M}
for which
X^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{\theta=\pi/2}=0
,
we also define
\vec{J}(x,y,\theta,t)=J^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)\Psi^{2}+J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)R\Psi^{2}
, where

J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=-\frac{1}{\cos(\theta)}X^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),\quad J^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=0.

(2.2.27)

Note that
\vec{J}
is also a Jacobi field; at
\theta=\pi/2

it is defined by taking the limit
\theta\to\pi/2
.

These Jacobi fields make sense for
all
maps
\vec{\Psi}
on a manifold

(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
that are adapted to the geodesic flow.
We note that
\vec{J}
has a natural interpretation when
\vec{\Psi}
is a
circle family:

Remark 2.5
.

We note that when
\vec{\Psi}
is a circle family the vector field
\vec{J}(x,y,\theta,t)
can defined also by

\displaystyle\vec{J}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\bigg|_{s=0}\gamma_{q(s),\zeta(s)}(t),

(2.2.28)

where for
(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
q(s)=\gamma_{\phi(x,y),R\xi(x,y,\theta)}(s)
and the vector
\zeta(s)\in S_{q(s)}M
is
the parallel translation of the vector
\xi(x,y,\theta)
along the geodesic

\gamma_{\phi(x,y),R\xi(x,y,\theta)}([0,s])
from
\phi(x,y)
to
q(s)
.

Definition 2.2.5
.

For notational brevity, when we deal with Riemannian manifold
(M_{0},g_{0})
below,
where the metric
g_{0}
will be taken to be in the form
(
2.2.3
), we will denote
\sqrt{(g_{0})_{yy}(x,y)}
. by
\alpha_{0}(x,y)
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

For the Circle front families
\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)

in a manifold
(M_{0},g_{0})
with metric
g_{0}=dx^{2}+(\alpha_{0}(x,y))^{2}dy^{2}
, the
Jacobi fields have the initial values

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692ptJ_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=1,\quad\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=0,

(2.2.29)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692ptX_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=-\cos(\theta),\quad\dot{X}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=0,

(2.2.30)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692ptY_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}={{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}}\sin(\theta),\ \dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}={}\kappa_{0}(x,y){\,}{{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}}

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt{{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=0,\quad\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)|_{t=0}=-1}

(2.2.31)

and the parallel components of these fields satisfy
J_{0}^{//}=0
,

X_{0}^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=\sin(\theta)
, and

Y_{0}^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)={{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}}\cos(\theta)
,

{\bf B}_{0}^{//}=0
, and
\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{//}=0
.

Proof.

We start by proving the commutation of rotation operator
R
and the covariant derivative. By
using the fact that the Christoffel symbols in the Riemannian normal
coordinates vanish at the origin, we see that
all vector fields
V
and
W
satisfy

\nabla_{V}(RW)=R\nabla_{V}W.

(2.2.32)

Indeed, we see that at any point, the formula (
2.2.32
) holds
for the orthogonal coordinate vector fields at the origin of the normal coordinates, and
thus for any vector fields at any point.

Recall that
R\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)
arises from
\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)
by rotation

by
\frac{\pi}{2}
clockwise,

R(\alpha_{0}^{-1}\partial_{y})=-\partial_{x},R\partial_{x}=\alpha_{0}^{-1}\partial_{y}
.
Recall also that by the definition of the Jacobi fields, we derive for
\vec{A}
equal to either
\vec{J}_{{0}},\vec{Y}_{{0}},\vec{X}_{{0}},\vec{\bf B}_{{0}}

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{\vec{A}(x,y,\theta,t)}\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t),R\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\rangle=\dot{A}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t).

Recalling that for the circle-front families at
t=0
satisfy
\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,0)=\xi(x,y,\theta,0)
,
and recall the first paragraph from Remark
2.2
, along with the formulas (
2.2.6
) and (
2.2.17
) linking
\partial_{x},\partial_{y}
with
\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2},R\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}
. These yield
our claims on
J^{\perp}_{0},X^{\perp}_{0},\dot{J}_{0},\dot{X}_{0},Y^{\perp}_{0}
immediately.
The claims on
{\bf B}_{0},\dot{\bf B}_{0}
are immediate from its definition, since at
t=0
the
\partial_{\theta}

derivative merely rotates
\vec{\Psi}^{2}_{{0}}
anti-clockwise, while keeping
\Psi^{1}_{{0}}
constant.

It remains to derive the claims for
\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}
.
By the definition of the geodesic curvature
\kappa_{0}
, the formula

\langle\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\nabla_{\partial_{y}}\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,0),R\vec{\Psi}_{{0}}^{2}(x,y,\theta,0)\rangle=\kappa_{0}(x,y).

(2.2.33)

is valid for
\theta=\pi/2
.
Since the covariant derivative commutes with the rotation
that maps the vector

\vec{\Psi}^{2}_{{0}}(x,y,\pi/2,0)
to
\vec{\Psi}^{2}_{{0}}(x,y,\theta,0)
,
we see that (
2.2.33
) for all
\theta
.
This yields the claim for
\dot{Y}^{\perp}_{0}
.
∎

2.3
A generalization:
Pseudo
-circlefront families.

Our next goal is to put down some properties of circlefront families, which are purely expressible in the
known
part of the manifold
\widetilde{M}
. These properties will allow us to define the notion of pseudo-circlefront families,
which is
more
general than circlefront families.

When
{J}^{\perp}_{A}(t)
and
{J}^{\perp}_{B}(t)
are the normal components of any two solutions of the
Jacobi equation (
2.2.21
), see also (
2.2.25
), we define the symplectic product

\bigg\{(J^{\perp}_{A}(t),\dot{J}^{\perp}_{A}(t)),(J^{\perp}_{B}(t),\dot{J}^{\perp}_{B}(t))\bigg\}:=\dot{J}^{\perp}_{A}(t){\,}J_{B}^{\perp}(t)-J_{A}^{\perp}(t){\,}\dot{J}^{\perp}_{B}(t).

(2.3.1)

The symplectic product (
2.3.1
) is constant in
t
as it is the Wroskian determinant for the equation
(
2.2.21
).
This and Lemma
2.2.1

imply that the symplectic products satisfy at the time
t=-T
.

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(J_{0}^{\perp},\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}),({\bf B}_{0}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{{t}})=1,

(2.3.2)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(Y_{0}^{\perp},\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}),({\bf B}_{0}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{{t}})={\alpha_{0}(x,y)}{\,}\sin(\theta),

(2.3.3)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(Y_{0}^{\perp},\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}),(J_{0}^{\perp},\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{{t}})={}\kappa_{0}(x,y){\,}\alpha_{0}(x,y).

(2.3.4)

We also recall that in Gaussian coordinates
x,y
,
the Gauss curvature at the point
x,y
is expressible in terms of the geodesic curvature
\kappa_{0}{(x,y)}
of level sets of
x

via the formula:

G_{\kappa_{0}}(x,y)=-(\partial_{x}\kappa_{0}(x,y)+(\kappa_{0}(x,y))^{2}).

(2.3.5)

We also recall that the flow-curves
\Psi^{1}_{0}(x,y,\pi/2,t)
in both
t
and
x
are the arc-length parameterized geodesics and moreover
\partial_{t}=\partial_{x}
(at that angle
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2})
. Now, the Jacobi field
{\bf B}_{0}

solves the Jacobi field equation along these integrals curves, with initial
data at
(x,y,\pi/2,t=0)
given as in (
2.2.29
).
This implies there exists an explicit link between

({\bf B}_{0},\dot{\bf B}_{0})(x_{*},y,\pi/2,t=-x_{*})
and the function
\kappa_{0}(x,y),x\in[0,x_{*}]
. We make this precise using the next definition:

Definition 2.3.1
.

We define the operator
Jac
which acts on functions
\kappa(x,y)
,
x\in[0,\epsilon_{{0}}],y\in[-\delta_{{0}},\delta_{{0}}]
and outputs a function over the same space:

\displaystyle{\hbox{\rm Jac}}\big[\kappa\big](x,y)=\bigg({\hbox{\rm Jac}}^{1}\big[\kappa\big](x,y),{\hbox{\rm Jac}}^{2}\big[\kappa\big](x,y)\bigg),

(2.3.6)

\displaystyle{\hbox{\rm Jac}}^{1}\big[\kappa\big](x,y)=b(x,y,t)|_{t=-x},

\displaystyle{\hbox{\rm Jac}}^{2}\big[\kappa\big](x,y)=\partial_{t}b(x,y,t)|_{t=-x}

where
b(x,y,t)
is the solution of the Jacobi-type equation

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt(\partial_{t}^{2}+{{\cal G}[\kappa]}(x+t,y))b(x,y,t)=0,\quad{-T<t<T},

(2.3.7)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptb(x,y,t)|_{t=0}=0,\quad\partial_{t}b(x,y,t)|_{t=0}=-1.

(2.3.8)

where
{{\cal G}[\kappa]}(x,y)=-(\partial_{x}\kappa(x,y)+(\kappa(x,y))^{2}).

As noted, for the Circle front family
\Psi_{0}

the function
b(x,y,t)={\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta_{0},t)
is the solution to equations (
2.3.7
)-(
2.3.8
)
with
{\kappa}=\kappa_{0}
, where
{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
is
the normal component of the Jacobi field (
2.2.22
) evaluated at
\theta_{0}=\pi/2

and
{\cal G}[{\kappa_{0}]}(x,y)
is the Gauss curvature of
g
at
(x,y)
. In particular:

{\hbox{\rm Jac}}\big[\kappa_{0}\big](x,y)=\bigg({\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg)\bigg|_{\theta=\pi/2,t=-x}.

(2.3.9)

Motivated by the fact that a Circle front family satisfies
the equations (
2.3.2
)- (
2.3.4
), (
2.3.9
) and that
\alpha_{0}
and
\kappa_{0}
satisfy (
2.2.4
),
we next define the concept of Pseudo-circle front family as a generalization of the Circle front family.

Definition 2.3.2
.

Consider a manifold
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
and a parameter space
{\rm Param}
as in (
2.2.19
).
A
{\cal C}^{m-1}
-smooth
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
map,
defined for
(x,y,

\theta,t)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}

that is adapted to
the geodesic flow of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
(see Definition
2.2.2
), is a Pseudo-circle front family on
\widetilde{M}
at time
t_{0}\in[{-2T},{0}]
with parameter functions
(\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y))

if the
fields
(
2.2.22
)-(
2.2.24
), that is,
\vec{J}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
\vec{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
\vec{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
given in Def.
2.2.3
for
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)

satisfy
the following properties:

1.

The symplectic products satisfy at the time
t=t_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(J^{\perp},\dot{J}^{\perp}),({\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=1,

(2.3.10)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(Y^{\perp},\dot{Y}^{\perp}),({\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})={\alpha(x,y)}{\,}\sin(\theta),

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg\{(Y^{\perp},\dot{Y}^{\perp}),(J^{\perp},\dot{J}^{\perp})\bigg\}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})={{}}\kappa(x,y){\,}\alpha(x,y).

(2.3.12)

2.

The fields (
2.2.22
)-(
2.2.24
) satisfy at the time
t=t_{0}

\begin{split}&X^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=\sin(\theta),\quad\dot{X}^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=0,\\
&Y^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=\cos(\theta)\alpha(x,y),\quad\dot{Y}^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=0,\\
&{\bf B}^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=0,\quad\dot{\bf B}^{//}(x,y,\theta,{t_{0}})=0.\end{split}

(2.3.13)

3.

At the angle
\theta=\pi/2
, the function

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
satisfies
for
(x,y,\pi/2)\in\Omega_{large}

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg|_{\theta=\pi/2,\ t=-x}=(\phi(0,y),\partial_{x})

(2.3.14)

4.

The functions
\kappa
and
{\bf B}^{\perp}
satisfy

\displaystyle{\hbox{\rm Jac}}\big[\kappa\big](x,y)=\bigg({\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg)\bigg|_{\theta=\pi/2,t=-x},

(2.3.15)

for all
x\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]
. The (non-linear) operator
Jac
is defined in (
2.3.6
).

5.

The functions
\alpha(x,y)
and
\kappa(x,y)
satisfy

\partial_{x}\alpha(x,y)={}\kappa(x,y){\,}\alpha(x,y).

(2.3.16)

We will call the functions
J^{\perp},Y^{\perp},{\bf B}^{\perp}
the
Jacobi data associated to the pseudo-circle
\Psi
. The functions
\alpha,\kappa
are the
associated parameters
. Sometimes to stress that a pseudo-circle family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
explicitly depends on the associated function
\alpha(x,y)
we will denote the pseudo-circlefront by
\bigg(\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t),\alpha(x,y)\bigg)

Remark 2.6
.

We note that since the property (
2.3.14
) holds for all
y
, we can differentiate that equation in
y
, we derive that pseudo-circle front family also satisfies that for all
x\in[0,\epsilon_{1}]
, the function

Y^{\perp}
satisfies at
\theta=\pi/2
,
t=-x

Y^{\perp}(x,y,\pi/2,-x)={\alpha}(0,y),\quad\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\pi/2,-x)=0.

(2.3.17)

Similarly the property (
2.3.14
) implies
for all
t

\begin{split}&X^{\perp}(x,y,\pi/2,t)=0,\quad\dot{X}^{\perp}(x,y,\pi/2,t)=0.\end{split}

(2.3.18)

Moreover, the condition on
{\bf B}
imposed
in the formula (
2.3.13
)
implies the co-normality condition

\langle\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t),\Psi^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\rangle=0.

(2.3.19)

Remark 2.7
.

We note that circle families for the metric
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
are automatically pseudo-circle families, when the functions
\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)
in the Definition
2.3.2
are
chosen to be the 1st and 2nd fundamental forms
\alpha_{0}(x,y),\kappa_{0}(x,y)
associated to the Gaussian coordinates of
\widetilde{g}
.

Remark 2.8
.

Just as restrictions of circlefront families to fixed
t=t_{*}
or
(x=x_{*},y=y_{*})
define
circle families
and
circlefronts
in Definition
2.2.1
, we can define
these same restrictions to define
pseudo-circlefronts
and

pseudo-circle families
.

An important observation is that the notion of
a Pseudo-circle front family is independent of the time
t_{0}
:

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
t_{0},\tilde{t}_{0}\in{[{-2T},{0}]}
.
A
{\cal C}^{m-1}
-smooth map
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t):\Omega_{large}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}{\to(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})}
(adapted to the geodesic flow),
is a Pseudo-circle front family at the time
t_{0}
if and only if is a Pseudo-circle front family
at the time
\tilde{t}_{0}
.

Proof.

As
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
is adapted to the geodesic
flow, that is, the curves
t\to\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)
are geodesics,
we see that the conditions 1 and 2 in the definition of the Pseudo-circle front family
are valid at some time
t_{0}
if and only those are valid at all times

t_{0}
. For the first condition this follows from the conservation of the symplectic product, as noted after (
2.3.1
). For
the second property, we recall the Jacobi equation (
2.2.21
), which shows that since the
\dot{X}^{//},\dot{Y}^{//},\dot{{\bf B}}^{//}
vanish at some time
t_{0}
, the values
X^{//},Y^{//},{\bf B}^{//}
are constant in
t
. Likewise, the same Jacobi equation implies that the
assumed vanishing of
X^{\perp},\dot{X}^{\perp}
at one time implies
their vanishing at all other times.
As the other conditions do not involve
t_{0}
, the claim follows.
∎

Because of Lemma
2.3.1
, we will below often say just that

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
is a Pseudo-circle front family meaning that it is a Pseudo-circle front family
at all times
t
.

Observe that the definition of a Pseudo-circle front family does not involve the lens data. We enhance the definition by requiring a pseudo-circle family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
to agree with lens data in the next definition.

Definition 2.3.3
.

Consider a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
with its extension

(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
near a point
p\in\partial M
where
its boundary is convex. Consider the circle-front family

\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
and its associated parameters

\alpha_{0}(x,y)
, as
constructed in the beginning of this section, with
(x,y,\theta,t)
defined
over the parameter space
\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},0]
.

Now, consider a
pseudo
-circle family
{\big(}\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t),\alpha(x,y)\big)
, defined over the same parameter space
\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},0]
.

We say that

{\big(}\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t),\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)\big)
,

(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
,
agrees at time
t_{0}=-T
with the lens data of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})

over
\Omega_{large}^{known}
if

(A1)

We have at time
t=-T

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,-T)={S_{\widetilde{M},M}^{-T}}(\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)),\quad\hbox{for all }(x,y,\theta)\in\overline{\Omega^{known}_{{large}}}.

(2.3.20)

(See Fig.
5
for a depiction).

(A2)

\alpha(x,y)=\alpha_{0}(x,y)
and

\kappa(x,y)=\kappa_{0}(x,y)
when
x=0
and
-\delta_{0}<y<\delta_{0}
and when

y=\pm\delta_{0}

and
x\in[0,\epsilon_{0}]
.

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta_{1},-T)
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta_{2},-T)
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta_{3},-T)
\widetilde{M}\setminus M
p
\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta_{3})
\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta_{1})
\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta_{2})
\partial M
M
p

Figure 5:
The condition (A1): The values
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,-T)

are required to agree with the scattering relation

S^{-T}_{\widetilde{M},M}(\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta))
, to ensure that

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,0)
agrees with
\vec{\Phi}(x,y,\theta)
.
This imposition thus “sees” the lens data, since

S^{-T}_{\widetilde{M},M}
is built out of that lens data, via the map defined by the dotted lines: The
black curves are geodesic segments in the “known manifold”
\widetilde{M}
. The red points are the entry points of these geodesic segments into the “unknown”
M
. The dotted lines are the portions of these geodesics in the “unknown”
M
.

Remark 2.9
.

Observe that for a Pseudo-circle front family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
at time
t_{0}=-T

the condition (A1), is equivalent to requiring that

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
satisfies

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t_{0})=S_{\widetilde{M},M}^{t_{0}}(\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)),\quad\hbox{for all }(x,y,\theta)\in\overline{\Omega^{known}_{large}}

(2.3.21)

at any time
t_{0}\in{[{{-2T}},-\frac{1}{2}T]}
,
using (
2.2.13
) and the fact that pseudo-circle fronts are also adapted to geodesic flow in
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
.

Moreover, since
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
is adapted to the geodesic flow of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
,
the condition (A1) at time
t_{0}=-T
, implies

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg|_{t=0}=\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta)\quad\hbox{for all $(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega_{large}^{known}$}.

(2.3.22)

(In Fig.
5
this means the depicted
\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta_{i})
equal
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta_{i},0)
).

2.4
Two manifolds with same lens data reduce to a pair of
circlefront and
pseudo-circlefront families on one manifold.

The main theorem of this paper, which will imply our local
Theorem
1
(and after some more work, all
the other ones also) is that on a given manifold

(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
, a pseudo-circle front
family which agrees (locally) with the lens data of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
is in fact
(locally) a circle front
family:

Theorem 4
.

Consider a manifold
(M,g)
, a point
p\in\partial M
where
\partial M
is
convex, and a local extension
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})

of
(M,g)
near
p
, and
consider the domains

\Omega_{small}
and
\Omega_{large}
in parameter space as in
(
2.2.15
) and (
2.2.5
). Let

(\Psi_{0},\alpha_{0})
be the circlefront family over

\Omega_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]
as in Definition

2.2.1
.

Let
\big(\vec{\Psi}{,\alpha}\big):\Omega_{small}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}\to S\widetilde{M}{\times\mathbb{R}}
be a Pseudo-circle front family
at the time
t_{*}=-T
; assume
(\vec{\Psi},\alpha)
agrees at
time
t=-T
with the lens data of
(M,g)
in the region

\Omega^{known}_{small}
around
p\in\partial M
, as in Definition
2.3.3
.
Then there are
\epsilon_{2}\in(0,\epsilon_{1})
and
\delta_{2}\in(0,\delta_{1})
such that
\vec{\Psi}:\Omega(\epsilon_{2},\delta_{2})\to S\widetilde{M}
is a Circle front family and
\alpha{(x,y)}=\alpha_{0}{(x,y)}
.

Proposition 2.4.1
.

Theorem
4
imples Theorem

1
.

The proof of this reduction is independent of the
rest of the paper; it also is the point where the knowledge of the scattering map (which implies local equality of lens data) is used.

Proof.

(of Proposition
2.4.1
) Recall (
2.1.1
) where
\psi_{e}
is introduced, and also the Definition
2.1.1
of
S^{t}_{\widetilde{M}_{j},M_{j}},j=1,2
.
Below, we assume that the point
p_{1}\in\partial\widetilde{M}_{1}
has a neighborhood

U\subset\widetilde{M}_{1}
such that
\psi_{e}:U\cap(\widetilde{M}_{1}\setminus M_{1})\to\psi_{e}(U)\cap(\widetilde{M}_{2}\setminus M_{2})
is an isometry and
the lens data on the manifold
M_{1}
near the point
p_{1}
is equal to the lens data of
M_{2}
.
Also, let
\phi_{1}(x,y)
and
\phi_{2}(x,y)
be Gaussian coordinates defined on subsets of

\widetilde{M}_{1}
and
\widetilde{M}_{2}
, respectively, that satisfy

\phi_{2}(x,y)=\psi_{e}(\phi_{1}(x,y))
for
(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega^{known}_{large}
.

Let
\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,t)
be the Circle front family on
(\widetilde{M}_{1},\widetilde{g}_{1})
associated to
coordinates
\phi_{1}(x,y)
with parameter functions

(\alpha_{0}^{(1)},\kappa_{0}^{(1)})
.
Similarly, let
\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}(x,y,\theta,t)
be the Circle front family on
(\widetilde{M}_{2},\widetilde{g}_{2})
associated to
coordinates
\phi_{2}(x,y)
with parameter functions
(\alpha_{0}^{(2)},\kappa_{0}^{(2)})
. We recall
that in the Gaussian coordinates defined by
\phi_{j}(x,y)
, the metric tensor
g_{j}

is equal to
dx^{2}+\alpha_{0}^{(j)}(x,y)^{2}dy^{2},j=1,2
.

As the lens data of
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
coincide,
the operators
S^{t}_{\widetilde{M}_{j},M_{j}},j=1,2

coincide in the sense that

S^{t}_{\widetilde{M}_{2},M_{2}}\circ(\psi_{e})_{*}=(\psi_{e})_{*}\circ S^{t}_{\widetilde{M}_{1},M_{1}}

(2.4.1)

on the set
{\xi}(\tilde{\Omega}_{large}^{known})\times[{-2T},0]
, where

\xi:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}^{known}\to S^{1}\widetilde{M}_{1}
be defined on manifold
(M_{1},g_{1})
using the formula
(
2.2.6
).

6
6

6

We recall that at the times
t
and points
(p,\xi)\in S(\widetilde{M}_{1}\setminus M_{1})
for which
\gamma^{\widetilde{M}_{1}}_{p,\xi}(t)\not\in\widetilde{M}_{1}\setminus M_{1}
,
we have defined the value of

to be an empty set, see (
2.1.2
)).

By the definition of the Circle front families,
the above implies

\displaystyle\hskip-51.21495pt\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}(x,y,\theta,0)=(\psi_{e})_{*}\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,0),\quad(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}^{known}.

(2.4.2)

By using that the lens data on the manifold
M_{1}
near the point

, (

) implies

\displaystyle\hskip-51.21495pt\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}(x,y,\theta,-T)=(\psi_{e})_{*}\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,-T),\quad(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}^{known}.

(2.4.3)

Using the Circle front family
\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}
on
\widetilde{M}_{2}
, we define a new vector field over
\widetilde{M}_{1}\times[-2T,0]
, given
over the parameter space

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)=E^{(1)}_{t+T}((\psi_{e}^{-1})_{*}(\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}(x,y,\theta,-T)),

(2.4.4)

for
(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}

Observe that above in (

By combining the formula (

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,-T)

(2.4.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,-T).

Hence, as
\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,t)

2.2.20
),
and

\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,0)=E_{T}^{(1)}(\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,-T))\in\widetilde{M}_{1}\setminus M_{1}^{int}
for

.

Moreover, using (

) and that the both the field

),
we see that for all

\displaystyle\hskip-8.53581pt\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)=\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,t),\quad\hbox{for $(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}^{known}$.}

(2.4.7)

We emphasize that at this moment we do not know whether

and

; in fact

As
\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{2})}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}\to S\widetilde{M}_{2}

\widetilde{M}_{2}
, we next prove that
\vec{\Psi}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}\to S\widetilde{M}_{1}

with the parameter functions

(\alpha_{0}^{(2)}(x,y),\kappa_{0}^{(2)}(x,y))
.

We consider a given point

\displaystyle(Y^{\perp}(t),\dot{Y}^{\perp}(t),J^{\perp}(t),\dot{J}^{\perp}(t),{\bf B}^{\perp}(t),\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}(t))

(2.4.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(Y^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t),\dot{Y}^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t),J^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t),\dot{J}^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t),{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t),\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}_{0,(M_{2})}(t))

at the time

(and

).
Hence, as the Jacobi fields for the family

(as in Definition

.

As the

) at

for all

.
As the families

.

For all

\Psi^{1}_{0,(M_{2})}(x,y,\pi/2,[-T,-x])\subset\widetilde{M}_{2}\setminus M_{2}^{int}

Hence, the family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)

in the Definition

2.3.2
.

Above, we have verified for
\vec{\Psi}
all conditions in the definition of a Pseudo-circle front family, i.e., that
\vec{\Psi}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times{[{-2T},{0}]}\to S\widetilde{M}_{1}

at the time

with the parameter functions

.

\vec{\Psi}

2.3.3
on the manifold
\widetilde{M}_{1}
. As

has a neighborhood

such that

{\big(}\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t),\alpha_{0}^{(2)}(x,y),\kappa_{0}^{(2)}(x,y)\big)

agrees at the time

with the lens data of
(M_{1},g_{1})
on

,

2.3.3
.

Hence
\vec{\Psi}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times\mathbb{R}\to S\widetilde{M}_{1}
is a Pseudo-circle front family on the manifold

\widetilde{M}_{1}
with associated parameter functions
(\alpha_{0}^{(2)},\kappa_{0}^{(2)})
and
\vec{\Psi}

agrees at the time
t=-T
with the lens data.
Then, Theorem
4
implies that
there are
\epsilon_{2}>0
and
\delta_{2}>0
such that for
(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega(\epsilon_{2},\delta_{2})
and

\displaystyle\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)=\vec{\Psi}_{0,(M_{1})}(x,y,\theta,t),

(2.4.10)

\displaystyle(\alpha_{0}^{(2)}(x,y),\kappa_{0}^{(2)}(x,y))=(\alpha_{0}^{(1)}(x,y),\kappa_{0}^{(1)}(x,y)).

(2.4.11)

In particular, the metric tensor
dx^{2}+(\alpha_{0}^{(1)}(x,y))^{2}dy^{2}

and the tensor
dx^{2}+(\alpha_{0}^{(2)}(x,y))^{2}dy^{2}
on
[0,\epsilon_{2})\times(-\delta_{2},\delta_{2})
coincide.
This means that
(\phi_{1}((-\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{2})\times(-\delta_{2},\delta_{2})),g_{1})
is isometric to

(\phi_{2}((\epsilon_{2},\epsilon_{2})\times(-\delta_{2},\delta_{2})),g_{2})
, that is,
p_{1}
has a neighborhood

U_{1}\subset\widetilde{M}_{1}
and
p_{2}
has a neighborhood

U_{2}\subset\widetilde{M}_{2}
such that
\psi_{e}
extends to an isometry
U_{1}\to U_{2}
.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

We note that we could derive the isometry of the two manifolds
also using (
2.4.10
) (instead of (
2.4.11
)) — the former equation implies that the two metrics

(M_{1},g_{1}),(M_{2},g_{2})
have distance functions that agree,
since the distance function can be reconstructed from
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,-T)
,
\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,-T)
,

(x,y,\theta)\in[0,\epsilon]\times[-\delta,\delta]\times[0,\pi]
.

In view of Proposition
2.4.1
, the rest of this paper until section

9
is devoted
to proving Theorem
4
.
From this point onwards
\Psi_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
will refer to
the circle-front family referred to in Theorem
4

over
\Omega_{small}\times[{-2T},{0}]
,
with associated parameters
\alpha_{0}(x,y),\kappa_{0}(x,y)
and
\big(J^{\perp}_{0},\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp},

Y^{\perp}_{0},

\dot{Y}^{\perp}_{0},{\bf B}_{0},\dot{\bf B}_{0}\big)(x,y,\theta,t)
.
The

pseudo
-circlefront family
\Psi(x,y,\theta,t)

and its associated parameters
\alpha(x,y)
etc. will lack the subscript
0
.

2.5
Outline of the proof of Theorem
2

We now outline the main argument for proving Theorem
4
.

Using a simple argument based on inverse function theorem
and that both

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
\vec{\Psi}_{0}(H(x,y,\theta),t)
are adapted
to the geodesic flow of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
we obtain
that there is a
C^{m-3}
-map

H:\Omega_{small}\to\Omega_{large}

such that

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)=\vec{\Psi}_{0}(H(x,y,\theta),t),

(2.5.1)

for
t\in[-2T,{0}]
.
We denote
H(x,y,\theta)=(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta))

and
F(x,y,\theta)=(a(x,y,\theta),b(x,y,\theta),c(x,y,\theta))
.
This
F
measures the difference between the circle family and the pseudo-circle family, measured over the common parameter space.

We will use the vector fields

\vec{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Psi(x,y,\theta,t)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\vec{Y}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Psi_{0}(x,y,\theta,t).

These fields define Jacobi fields along the curves

t\to\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
t\to\Psi^{1}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
, respectively.

We denote the normal components, see (
2.2.25
) by

Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
Y^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
and define the differences

\displaystyle\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t)=Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-Y^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t),

\displaystyle\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{t}Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-\partial_{t}Y^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t),

\displaystyle\delta\alpha(x,y)=\alpha(x,y)-\alpha_{0}(x,y),\quad\delta\kappa(x,y)=\kappa(x,y)-\kappa_{0}(x,y).

Analogously, we define (differences of) the normal components of the Jacobi fields
\vec{J},\vec{{\bf B}}
via
\delta J(x,y,\theta,t)=J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-J^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
\delta{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,t)={\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
.

The very definition of the Jacobi fields
\vec{J},\vec{Y}
imply that the evolutions of the circle and pseudo-circle fronts in
x
and in
y
(given by formulas (
4.0.12
), (
3.0.2
) below) are captured by the Jacobi fields
\vec{J},\vec{Y}
,
and most importantly by the normal components just defined.

The link between the parameters
\delta J
etc (which capture the first derivatives of the circle and pseudo-circle fronts) and the derivatives of the “difference”
F(x,y,\theta)
function introduced just above follows from
the simple chain rule

D\vec{\Psi}=D\vec{\Psi}_{0}\circ(Id+DF)
. This will imply that the first order
\partial_{x},\partial_{y}
derivatives of
F
in are expressible precisely in terms of

\delta J
and
\delta Y

This leads to a system of equations
(
4.0.5
), (
4.0.6
) on
F

which is schematically in the
form:

A\cdot\partial_{x}F=m(\delta J|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{J}|_{t=-T},F),\quad A\cdot\partial_{y}F=m(\delta Y|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T},F).

(Here and below
m(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,\dots)
will be a linear function on the input parameters, with coefficients which are allowed to
depend both
\vec{\Psi},\vec{\Psi}_{0}
and their associated parameters
\alpha,\alpha_{0}
and up to two derivatives thereof).

A first application of the conservation of the symplectic products is that
\delta J|_{t=-T},

\delta\dot{J}|_{t=-T},

\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}
can be expressed with respect to
\delta{\bf B}|_{t=-T},

\delta\dot{\bf B}|_{t=-T},

\delta Y|_{t=-T},

\delta\alpha,

\partial_{x}\delta\alpha
— see Lemma
4.3.1
. As we will see, Lemma
4.1.1
implies that
\delta{\bf B},\delta\dot{\bf B}
are expressible in terms of
\partial_{\theta}F
,
\delta Y
,
\delta\alpha
, and
F
, see (
4.3.1
).

This will imply that the functions
F(x,y,\theta),\delta Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),

\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),
and
\delta\alpha

satisfy a system of partial differential equations over the parameter space.

This leads to a schematic system of equations:

(2.5.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}F+{C}(x,y,\theta)\partial_{\theta}F={m}(F,\delta Y|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T},\delta\alpha,\partial_{x}\delta\alpha),

(2.5.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}F={m}(F,\delta Y|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T},\delta\alpha),

at the points
(x,y,\theta)\in{\Omega_{small}}
, where
\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y}
are evaluated at
t=-T
. So far,
we have not obtained a closed system of equations — the RHSs of the above, with their dependence on

\delta\alpha
and
\delta Y|_{t=-T}
can be seen as source terms.

We obtain the second type of equations in system by using the commutation of the vector fields
\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{\theta}
and
\partial_{t}
,
pushing the commutation relations forward by the maps
\vec{\Psi}
and
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
, that is, e.g.,

\displaystyle[\vec{X},\vec{\bf B}]=(\vec{\Psi})_{*}[{\partial}_{x},{\partial}_{\theta}]=0,

(2.5.4)

see Lemma
3.0.1
.
We combine these with the sympletic relations
(
2.3.10
)-(
2.3.12
). In this way, we obtain (on the precise formulation,
see (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.4
) below)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}\,\delta Y={m}(F,\partial_{\theta}F,\delta Y,\delta\alpha,\partial_{x}\delta\alpha,\partial_{y}\delta\alpha,\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\delta\alpha),

(2.5.5)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}\,\delta\dot{Y}={m}(F,\partial_{\theta}F,\delta\dot{Y},\delta\alpha,\partial_{x}\delta\alpha,\partial_{y}\delta\alpha,\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\delta\alpha),

(2.5.6)

at the points
(x,y,\theta,t)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{{s}mall}\times(-2T,0)
and the 1st order partial differential
operator

\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}=-\partial_{t}+V_{1}\partial_{x}+V_{2}\partial_{y}+V_{3}\partial_{\theta}
is an explicit vector field in over
\tilde{\Omega}_{small}\times(-2T,0)
which arises naturally via the commutation relations and the
symplectic products (
2.3.10
), (
2.3.11
), (
2.3.12
) — see (
3.1.7
) for its precise form.

Thus, combining (
2.5.2
), (
2.5.3
) with (
2.5.5
), (
2.5.6
)
we obtain a system of first order equations on the variables
F,(\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y})
, where there are extra functions
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
7
7

7

Recall these are the differences of parameter functions

\alpha
and
\kappa
associated to Pseudo-circle front family
\vec{\Psi}
and those associated to Circle front family
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
.
and derivatives thereof which for now can only be seen as forcing terms.

We will then use the properties 3,4, and 5 of the pseudo-circlefront family to
obtain a further equation that couples
\delta\alpha
with
F,(\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y})
; this will provide (in principle) a closed system of equations: We consider
the functions

\delta\alpha(x,y)
and
\delta\kappa(x,y)
.
implies that
that the functions
\delta\alpha(x,y)
and
\delta\kappa(x,y)
satisfy a (non-linear) Volterra equations (on the precise formulation,
see Lemma
8.1.2
and formula (
8.3.22
) below)

\delta\alpha(x,y)+\int_{0}^{x}a(x,y,t-x){\,}\delta\alpha(t,y)dt={m}(F|_{\theta={\pi/2}},\partial_{\theta}F|_{\theta={\pi/2}}).

(2.5.7)

Thus, appending (
2.5.7
) to the system (
2.5.2
)-(
2.5.6
) we obtain what in principle is a closed system of first order equations on the parameters
F,\delta Y,\delta\alpha
.
8
8

8

Note that
the coefficients of the partial differential equations (
2.5.2
)-(
2.5.6
) and (
2.5.7
)
depend on
\widetilde{g}_{1}
and
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
. In particular, they are a nonlinear system in
(\vec{\Psi},\alpha)
.

To obtain the desired vanishing of all the parameters
F,\delta Y,\delta\alpha
, this system must be enhanced to include vanishing initial data.

The condition (A1) and Remark
2.9
imply that
\delta Y^{\perp}
and
\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}
,
\delta\alpha
satisfy on
\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}=\{x=0,y\in[-\delta_{1},\delta_{1}],\theta\in[0,\pi]\}\bigcap\{x\in[0,\epsilon_{1}],y=\pm\delta_{1},\theta\in[0,\pi]\}
the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\delta Y^{\perp}|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}}=0,\quad\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}},\quad{F|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}}=0,\quad\delta\alpha|_{\tilde{\Gamma}_{1}}=0}.

(2.5.8)

The equations (
2.5.2
)-(
2.5.7
)
are considered as a coupled system for
(F(x,y,\theta),

\delta Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),

\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),

\delta\alpha(x,y))
,
along with the vanishing of all these parameters (
2.5.8
).

Having derived these equations, our main task is to derive that this system of functions then satisfies an energy inequality (for a suitable notion of energy for this complicated system of 1st order equations),
and using the vanishing initial and boundary values we show that this energy much vanish for small enough
x
, by virtue of a Gronwall inequality; this will yield
F=0
.
Hence,
H=Id+F
in (
2.5.1
) is the identity map and
\alpha(x,y)=\alpha_{0}(x,y)
. This will imply Theorem

4
.

While the system of evolution equations (
2.5.2
)–(
2.5.6
), the Volterra equation (
2.5.7
)
and the boundary condition (
2.5.8
) must be studied
in unison
to derive the desired vanishing of all parameters
F,\delta Y,\delta\alpha
,
it is worth commenting some of these equations
in isolation:
In particular, consider separately the equations
(
2.5.2
)-(
2.5.3
) on
F
(when we take
\delta Y,\delta\alpha
as given), and then (
2.5.5
)-(
2.5.6
) when we take
F,\delta\alpha
as given and finally (
2.5.7
), when we take
F,\delta Y
as given. The purpose is to discuss the
type
of analysis needed in to control the quantities
F
,
(\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y})
and
\delta\alpha
respectively. Solving for the set of equations (
2.5.2
), (
2.5.3
) would be approached as a first order system of equations, which (if one were to set the RHS to zero) would admit an estimate for
F

with no loss of derivative, however on suitable function spaces with a weight that vanishes at
\theta=0,\theta=\pi
. (On the boundaries
\{x=0\}
, and
\{y=\pm\delta_{1}\}
one does not have a degenerating weight — this is not required since
we know
that
F
vanishes on those boundaries). As we will note further down in the proof, the estimate one obtains is not of Carleman type
— the weight function serves a different purpose from that in a Carleman estimate.

The equations (
2.5.5
), (
2.5.6
) (taking
\partial F
,
\delta\kappa
as given)
are transport equations on
\delta Y
. The key thing is whether flowing along
\vec{\cal V}_{{0}}
