takes us to the boundary where
\delta Y
is known to vanish.
This is one of the two settings where the convexity of
\partial M
is
used in an essential way.
Finally, the treatment of equation (
2.5.7
) is via energy estimates for an integral operator
of order zero, acting on functions over line segments of length
\epsilon_{1}
;
\epsilon_{1}
needs to be taken suitably small to guarantee the invertability of such an operator. We obtain control of
\delta\alpha
on these intervals in terms of
\partial|_{\theta=\pi/2}F
. Once we study the entire coupled system, this quantity (and in fact, a finite number of its derivatives, still at
\theta=\pi/2
) will be controlled via a Sobolev trace theorem.

We should note that our main unknowns involve
functions of three variables

x,y,\theta
, e.g., (
F,\partial_{\theta}F
), of four variables, e.g.,

\big(\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t),

\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)\big)
, and of functions
\delta\alpha
of only the two variables
x,y
. In spite of this, the system of equations we have on the different functions (
F,\delta Y,\delta\alpha
) allows us to combine the separate
estimates that we obtain on each of these functions (and on a suitable number of their derivatives) into one “final” energy-type
estimate on up to two derivatives of
F
, in a suitably weighted Sobolev-type space. This ultimately yields our uniqueness result.
It is hoped that the nature of the estimates will also imply a stability of the reconstruction–this is left to the future.

A key point
of our approach is that we do not consider integrals of the unknown functions over the geodesics between boundary points,
which is the widely used technique in the study of the lens and boundary rigidity, see e.g.
[
30
,
55
,
58
,
61
,
68
,
72
,
67
]
.
Instead, we are naturally led (through the equations (
2.5.5
), (
2.5.6
) that we derived) to consider the integrals
over the flow curves of the vector field
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}

that connect an interior point
q=\phi(x,y)\in M
to the boundary. It is worth digressing for a moment to connect this novel vector field
\vec{\cal V}_{0}

with a class of vector fields that has already appeared in the literature, the
thermostats
.
We first recall the geodesic vector field
\vec{\mathcal{X}}
in
TM^{2}
:

Using the local coordinates
(x^{1},x^{2})
on
M

and the associated coordinates
(x^{1},x^{2},\xi^{1},\xi^{2})
for the point
(x^{1},x^{2},\xi^{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}+\xi^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}})

of
TM
, we can write the vector field
\vec{\mathcal{X}}
on
TM
as

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{X}}=\xi^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}-\xi^{j}\xi^{k}\Gamma^{p}_{jk}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{p}}.

Note that
\vec{\mathcal{X}}
can be expressed as a vector field on the unit circle bundle
SM
where we can use coordinates

(x^{1},x^{2},\theta)=(x,y,\theta)
, see
[
44
]
.

On the other hand, our vector field
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}

expressed in the coordinates
(x,y,\theta,t)
admits the expression:

\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}=-\partial_{t}+\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}+\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\partial_{y}+\kappa_{0}(x,y)\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}.

In particular
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}
can be written as
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}=-\partial_{t}+\vec{V}_{0}
.

It turns out that one can calculate that
\vec{A}=\vec{\mathcal{X}}-\vec{V}_{0}
is only a multiple of
\partial_{\theta}
. Vector fields with this property are called “thermostats”, see
[
19
,
44
,
44
]
. The term
\vec{A}
can be seen as introducing a modification to the flow of
\vec{V}_{0}
relative to the geodesic flow, and can thus be seen as an acceleration term, induced by a magnetic field. The flow curves of
\vec{V}_{0}
can thus be seen as generalization of geodesics with external magnetic field
\vec{\mathcal{A}}
. Thermostat fields have earlier been used in scattering rigidity
to consider determination of the jet of the metric on the boundary, see

[
7
]
.

Finally, let us compare the above methods to the microlocal techniques used extensively to study the boundary rigidity problems.
The microlocal studies
use strongly the properties of the linearized inverse problem and the mapping properties of the linear geodesic

X
-ray transform.

As we explained earlier, our methods are
direct
methods, which treat the non-linear problem;
our main parameters to prove rigidity (uniqueness) for are the Jacobi fields
\vec{Y},\vec{J},\vec{{\bf B}}
and the metric components
\alpha
, which satisfy a complicated algebraic and differential equations
which couple them — this is the content of Definition
2.3.2
.
The lens data is closely related to the Jacobi fields since these are the first variations of geodesics. The matching of the lens data is the content of Definition
2.3.3
. It is important to note that while the system of equations that we study is non-linear, since we are only interested in showing rigidity (uniqueness) in this paper, the system that we actually study (on
differences
of circles and pseudo-circles, and the differences
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
of their associated parameters) appears
linear
. This is only because we see the coefficients of the resulting equations (which depend on these same quantities) as smooth coefficients. Finally, we mention that even the linearization of our problem (the rigidity of the X-ray transform near near a convex boundary point) is not solved --- this paper (and its methods)
only addresses the non-linear question
9
9

9

We thank Gunther Uhlmann for pointing this connection out to us.
.

In this, our techniques differ to those used in the studies based on the linear geodesic
X
-ray transform.

2.6
Notations and conventions.

Throughout the paper
m
is taken to be any integer
\geq 5
. It measures the smoothness of the metrics
g_{0},g_{1}
, which are assumed to lie in
{\cal C}^{m}
.
We use
C,C_{0},C_{1},\dots
to denote generic constants which value may change from one formula to another.
These generic constants depend on
the domain
\Omega_{large}
, metric
g
on
\Omega_{large}
, the family
\vec{\Psi}_{0}

and the a priori bounds for (
2.6.3
). In particular
we emphasize that for constants which depend on the parameters
\lambda
and
M

whose values are chosen at the end of the paper we use notations
C(\lambda)
,
C(\lambda,M)
,
etc.

Below, let
c_{0},c_{1},C_{0}>0
be such that
\kappa_{0},\alpha_{0}
in the Gaussian coordinates
satisfy
for all
(x,y)\in\Omega_{large}

\displaystyle c_{0}<|\kappa_{0}(x,y)|<c_{1},\quad|\frac{\partial_{y}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)^{2}}|\leq C_{0}.

(2.6.1)

Vector fields defined over domains in
(x,y,\theta)
coordinates or
(x,y,\theta,t)
coordinates will usually be decorated by
\vec{}
. We will denote by and
[\vec{A},\vec{B}]=\vec{A}\vec{B}-\vec{B}\vec{A}
the commutator of vector fields (or operators).

On the other hand, we sometimes use the term “Jacobi field” to denote a genuine Jacobi field
\vec{V}(t)
defined along a geodesic
\gamma(t)
, but also sometimes to just refer to the normal component
V^{\perp}(t)
of that vector field — see Definition
2.2.3
.

A note on function spaces that involve functions of
x,y,\theta
over domains
A
that depend on parameters
x,y,\theta
: We let
dV_{x,y,\theta}=dxdyd\theta
. Then for any
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

we let
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{a}(A)}
be the norm:

\|f\|_{L^{2}_{a}}=\sqrt{\int_{A}{|f(x,y,\theta)|^{2}}\cdot\sin^{a}(\theta){dV_{x,y,\theta}}}.

Similar notation is used for functions
f(y,\theta)
depending on
(y,\theta)
.
Further down, we will be considering hypersurfaces in the
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x,y,\theta}
space, given by level sets of a function
z(x,y,\theta)
the volume form that
{dV_{x,y,\theta}}
induces on such hypersurfaces will be denoted by
dS_{z}
.

We also introduce some conventions on subscripts and supersripts:
Subscripts
{}_{x},{}_{y}
will stand for parameters on which a function depends. (For example, we write
{\cal C}_{x,y}(t)
instead of
{\cal C}(t,x,y)
to stress that the variable
t
plays a different role to the variables
x,y
). In contrast, superscripts
{}^{I},^{xy,yy}
are
labels
.
For example, we will introduce further down the short-hand notation

\delta Y^{I}(x,y,\theta,t)={\partial^{I}_{\theta}}\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t).

(2.6.2)

This convention will extend to other key functions
\delta J^{I}={\partial^{I}_{\theta}}\delta J
,

\delta\dot{\bf B}^{I}={\partial^{I}_{\theta}}\delta\dot{\bf B}
, and
F^{I}={\partial^{I}_{\theta}}F
later on.

We often employ the following convention: When we have a function
of many variables (say
L(x,y,\theta)
) and we freeze one variable (say

x
), we will write
L(x_{*},y,\theta)
to stress that the variable
x

is given the fixed value
x_{*}
, while the others are allowed to vary.
This is especially useful in evaluations of certain derivatives below.
For example
\partial|_{x=x_{*}}L(x,g(x),\theta_{*})
is

different
from
\partial|_{x=x_{*}}L(x,g(x_{*}),\theta_{*})
.

Also, when we consider a Pseudo-circle front family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
on manifold
M
,
with parameter functions
\alpha(x,y)
and
\kappa(x,y)
that satisfy the conditions (A1) and (A2) in
Definition
2.3.3
.
We will assume that

\alpha,\alpha_{0}\in C^{m-{2}}({[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}}]\times[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}])
and
\kappa,\kappa_{0}\in C^{m-2}({[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}}]\times[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}])
and
that
\epsilon_{0}>0
is so small that these satisfy

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\|\kappa-\kappa_{0}\|_{C^{m-2}({[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}}]\times[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}])}<1,

(2.6.3)

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219pt\|\alpha-\alpha_{0}\|_{C^{m-{2}}({[-\epsilon_{0},\epsilon_{0}}]\times[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}])}<1.

(Note that the assumed
{\cal C}^{m}
-smoothness of the metrics
g,g_{0}
, along with the fact that they are known to agree on the boundaries
\partial M,\partial M_{0}
imply that such an
{\epsilon_{0}}>0
can always be found.) This bound will be implicitly assumed throughout the paper. In fact many of the constants in inequalities throughout the paper implicitly depend on this bound.

Finally, in our argument
there are some key parameters,
\epsilon,\zeta,\lambda
, that will be restricted at successive steps.
For example, to bound
\lambda>0
,
we will be successively imposing bounds
\lambda\leq\lambda^{(1)},\lambda\leq\lambda^{2)},\dots
:
In the claim of Lemma
7.1.1
for certain integral operators,
is shown to be valid when
\lambda<\lambda^{(1)}
.
Later, in Proposition
7.3.1
, we show using
Lemma
7.1.1
that when

\lambda<\lambda^{(2)}
, certain equations have solutions.
The bounds
\lambda^{(j)},\zeta^{(j)}
and
\epsilon^{(j)}
are numbered in order of appearance
to make it easier to find where those are introduced. To clarify the presentation, we have chosen the indices of the bounds so that if two bounds are introduced at the same step, e.g., the bounds
\lambda^{(2)}
and
\zeta^{(2)}
, the bounds have the same super-index. Because of this convention, we will jump over some numbers in the
indexing, but hope that this convention will clarify the presentation.

3
Evolution equations for a pseudo-circle family, in terms
of its associated Jacobi data.

We will now be considering pseudo-circle families
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
, and pay particular attention to the fields
\vec{\Psi}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot,-T)
at the fixed time
t=-T
. We first derive how the evolution of this family in the
x,y
variables is captured by its associated Jacobi fields
J,Y
, and also the first variation
of each pseudo-circle
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,-T)
in
\theta
is captured by
{\bf B}
. The next goal is to derive
that in view of the properties imposed on pseudo-circle families, the functions
J,\dot{J},Y,\dot{Y}
can be solved for in terms of
{\bf B},\dot{\bf B},\alpha,\kappa
.

Let
\vec{J},\vec{Y},
and
\vec{\bf B}
be the Jacobi fields
associated to a pseudo-front family
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
see Definition
2.2.3
. A consequence the definitions
of these fields are the following formulas:

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,{t})=\sin(\theta){\,}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})-\cos(\theta)J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t}),\\
&\nabla_{\partial_{x}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})=0\cdot\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})-\cos(\theta)\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})\end{split}

(3.0.1)

and

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,{t})=\cos(\theta)\alpha(x,y){\,}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})+Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t}),\\
&\nabla_{\partial_{y}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})=0\cdot\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})+\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})\end{split}

(3.0.2)

Also, by definition of the Jacobi fields
{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t})
,

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,{t})=0\cdot\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})+{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t}),

(3.0.3)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\nabla_{\partial_{\theta}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})=0\cdot\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t})+\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{t}){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,{t}).

Above,
\nabla
stands for the pull-back of the Levi-Civita connection of
(\widetilde{M}_{,}\widetilde{g}_{0})
onto the parameter space
{\rm Param}
by the map
\vec{\Psi}
.

We will be treating the variables
x,y
as
the “evolutionary” variables, in the sense that
\theta
parametrizes (pseudo)-circles themselves, whereas
x,y
parametrize
the family
of (pseudo-)circles.
We also note that moving in the
\partial_{x},{\pm}\partial_{y}
directions from the boundary
\Gamma
in parameter space
penetrates
into the unknown region
\partial\Omega_{large}^{unknown}={\partial(\Omega_{large}\setminus\Omega^{known}_{large})}
.

In view of formulas (
3.0.1
), (
3.0.2
), the evolution in
x,y
of the pseudo-circle
family at any fixed
t=t_{*}

is captured by the quantities
J^{\perp},\dot{J}^{\perp},Y^{\perp},\dot{Y}^{\perp}
.

Our first goal in this section will be to obtain precise formulas for the Jacobi data
J^{\perp},\dot{J}^{\perp},Y^{\perp},\dot{Y}^{\perp}
at our preferred time
t=-T
in terms of the functions

\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)
, as well as
{\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}
.
Morally this can be seen as capturing the evolution in
x,y
of our pseudo-circle
front family in terms of the parameters

\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)
,
{\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}
on which we will later obtain more control.

3.0.1
Auxiliary relation between
{\bf B}^{{\perp}}
,
Y^{{\perp}}
, and
J^{{\perp}}
.

We obtain a first useful formula that connects the three Jacobi fields
{\bf B}
,
Y
, and
J
.
This formula relies on the commutations between the vector fields
\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{\theta}
in parameter space, and the push-forward of these formulas via the map
\vec{\Psi}
.

Lemma 3.0.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\partial_{y}{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{\theta}Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){+}{{{\alpha}}(x,y)\cos(\theta)}\,\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t),

(3.0.4)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\partial_{x}{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=\sin(\theta)J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sin(\theta){\,}\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t).

(3.0.5)

Proof.

We work on the manifold
\widetilde{M}
, via the diffeomorphism
\Psi
from the parameter space to
\widetilde{M}
.
As
\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1}||R\Psi^{2}
by (
2.3.19
),
and
\nabla_{y}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\perp R\Psi^{2}
,
we see that

\langle\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{y}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle=0.

Similarly,
\langle\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{x}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle=0
.
Below, we denote
\vec{\Psi}^{2}={\Psi}_{*}(\partial_{t})

just by
\partial_{t}
.
We observe that the derivatives
\nabla_{\partial_{y}}
,
\nabla_{\partial_{\theta}}
and the rotation
R
pairwise commute and
R^{-1}=-R
.
Using these and the fact that
\dot{{\bf B}}^{//}=0
,
we see that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}(R\vec{\Psi}^{2})=R\nabla_{\partial_{\theta}}\partial_{t}=R\nabla_{\partial_{t}}\partial_{\theta}=R\nabla_{\partial_{t}}({\bf B}^{\perp}R\Psi^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad=-({\partial_{t}}{\bf B}^{\perp})\Psi^{2}+{\bf B}^{\perp}R\nabla_{\Psi^{2}}\Psi^{2}={-}{\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}\Psi^{2}.}

Hence, we have

\langle\nabla_{y}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{\theta}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle=-\langle\nabla_{y}\Psi^{1},\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}\Psi^{2}\rangle={-}Y^{//}\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}

(3.0.6)

and similarly,

\langle\nabla_{x}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{\theta}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle={-}X^{//}\dot{{\bf B}}^{\perp}.

By formulas (
3.0.1
) and (
3.0.2
),

X^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=\sin(\theta)
and
Y^{//}(x,y,\theta,t)=\cos(\theta)\,\alpha(x,y)
.
We invoke the above formulas in the next calculation:

\begin{split}\partial_{y}{\bf B}^{\perp}&=\partial_{y}\langle\partial_{\theta}\Psi^{1},R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle\\
&=\langle\nabla_{y}\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1},R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle+\langle\nabla_{\theta}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{y}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle\\
&=\langle\nabla_{\theta}\nabla_{y}\Psi^{1},R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle+0\\
&=\nabla_{\theta}\langle\nabla_{y}\Psi^{1},R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle-\langle\nabla_{y}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{\theta}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle\\
&=\partial_{\theta}Y^{\perp}{+}{\cos(\theta){\,}{{\alpha}}}{\,}\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}.\end{split}

(3.0.7)

This shows (
3.0.4
).
Similarly, by using (
3.0.1
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{x}{\bf B}^{\perp}&=\nabla_{\theta}\langle\nabla_{x}\Psi^{1},R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle-\langle\nabla_{x}\Psi^{1},\nabla_{\theta}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\rangle\\
&=-{\partial_{\theta}(\cos(\theta)J^{\perp}){+}\sin(\theta){\,}\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}}\\
&={\sin(\theta)J^{\perp}-\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}J^{\perp}{+}\sin(\theta){\,}\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}}.\end{split}

(3.0.8)

This proves (
3.0.5
).
∎

3.1
Solving for
Y^{\perp}
in terms of
\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)
,
{\bf B}^{\perp},\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}
:
The “thermostatic” vector field
\vec{\cal V}

The goal of this subsection is to derive Proposition
3.1.1
below. We start with a preparatory Lemma.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

For Pseudo-circle front families the following is true:
At all points
(x,y,\theta,t)
we have

\begin{split}&-\cos^{2}(\theta){\,}{\alpha(x,y)}{\,}\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+\partial_{x}Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&+\cos(\theta){\,}\partial_{y}J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-\sin(\theta){\,}\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=0.\end{split}

(3.1.1)

Proof.

We prove the transport equation using the commutation of the vector fields

{\partial}_{x}
,
{\partial}_{y}
, and
{\bf T}=\partial_{t}

in the parameter space
\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]

and formulas (
3.0.1
)
and (
3.0.2
)
which capture the push-forward of these commuting vector fields on the manifold
M
.

As
(\vec{\Psi})_{*}[{\partial}_{x},{\bf T}]=0
, letting
\nabla
be the pull-back of the Levi-Civita connection of
g
via the map
\vec{\Psi}
,
we derive that:

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\partial}_{x}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\partial}_{x}}{\bf T}=\nabla_{\bf T}{\partial_{x}}

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{\Psi}^{2}}\bigg(\sin(\theta)\vec{\Psi}^{2}-{\cos(\theta)}{\,}J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\,R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\cos(\theta)}\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(t,\theta).

Similarly, as
(\vec{\Psi})_{*}[{\partial}_{y},{\bf T}]=0
,
we see that

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\partial}_{y}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\partial}_{y}}{\bf T}=\nabla_{\bf T}{{\partial}_{y}}

(3.1.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\vec{\Psi}^{2}}\bigg(\cos(\theta){{\alpha}}(x,y)\vec{\Psi}^{2}+Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\,R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(t,\theta)(x,y).

Since the rotation
R
and the covariant derivative commute and

RR\vec{\Psi}^{2}=-\vec{\Psi}^{2}
, the above equations (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
) also imply:

\begin{split}\nabla_{{\partial}_{x}}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)&=R\nabla_{{\partial}_{x}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&={\cos(\theta)}\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t),\\
\nabla_{{\partial}_{y}}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)&=R\nabla_{{\partial}_{y}}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&=-\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\,\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t).\end{split}

(3.1.4)

Using (
2.2.32
) and the formulas (
3.0.1
) and (
3.0.2
)
we derive the following two equations

\begin{split}&\nabla_{{\partial}_{x}}{\partial}_{y}=\nabla_{\partial_{x}}\bigg(\cos(\theta){{\alpha}}(x,y){\,}\ \vec{\Psi}^{2}+Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\bigg)\\
=&\bigg[{\cos(\theta){\,}}\alpha(x,y){\cdot}{(-1)}{\cos(\theta){\,}}\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+\partial_{x}Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg]{\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&+\bigg[\cos(\theta)\partial_{x}{{\alpha}}+Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}{\cos(\theta)}\dot{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg]{\,}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t),\\
&\nabla_{{\partial}_{y}}{\partial}_{x}=\nabla_{\partial_{y}}\bigg(\sin(\theta)\vec{\Psi}^{2}-\cos(\theta){\,}J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\,R\vec{\Psi}^{2}\bigg)\\
=&\bigg[-\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+\sin(\theta){\,}\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg]{\,}R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&+\bigg[\cos(\theta)J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t){\,}\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg]{\,}\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)\end{split}

(3.1.5)

We consider the equation
(\vec{\Psi})_{*}[{\partial}_{x},{\partial}_{y}]=0
and compare the coefficients multiplying

R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x,y,\theta,t)
. This yields the transport equation (
3.1.1
).
∎

To now derive the transport equation for
Y^{\perp}
below,
we need relations that the Jacobi fields
Y^{\perp},J^{\perp},{\bf B}^{\perp}
satisfy. To this end, we point out that
on a two dimensional manifold, the Jacobi fields on a given geodesic that are normal to it
form a linear space of dimension 2. This implies that the functions
J^{\perp},Y^{\perp},
and
{\bf B}^{\perp}

satisfy a linear relation, and simple algebraic computations show that the
symplectic equations (
2.3.10
)-(
2.3.12
) provide such a relation (see Appendix A,
section
11
for details):

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt{J}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{1}{\alpha(x,y)\sin(\theta)}{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+{}\frac{\kappa(x,y)}{\sin(\theta)}{\bf B}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t).

(3.1.6)

Below, we will use the following vector field
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha,\kappa}

and function
P_{\alpha}
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

We denote

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha,\kappa}=(-\cos^{2}(\theta)-\sin^{2}(\theta))\partial_{t}+\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha}}\partial_{y}+{}\cos(\theta){\,}\kappa\partial_{\theta}

\displaystyle{=-\partial_{t}+\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha}}\partial_{y}+{}\cos(\theta){\,}\kappa\partial_{\theta},}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptP_{\alpha}=\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}\bigg(\frac{1}{{\alpha(x,y)}}\bigg).

(3.1.7)

For

the “background” metric
g_{0}
with associated parameters
\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}
, we write
\vec{\cal V}_{0}
instead of
\vec{\cal V}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}
, for notational simplicity.

We also use
the time-independent vector field

\vec{V}_{0}={\pi}_{*}\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
, that is the space-component of
the vector field
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
, where
{\pi}_{*}:\Omega_{large}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

is the embedding
{\pi}:(x,y,\theta,t)\to(x,y,\theta)
. That is,

\displaystyle\vec{V}_{0}=\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}}\partial_{y}+{}\cos(\theta){\,}\kappa_{0}(x,y)\partial_{\theta}.

(3.1.8)

We then claim:

Proposition 3.1.1
.

For any
pseudo-circle front
\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
the function
Y^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)

satisfies the following transport equation:

\begin{split}\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{\alpha,\kappa}{{Y}}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+P_{\alpha}{{Y}}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=-{}\cos(\theta){{\bf B}}^{\perp}{\,}\partial_{y}\kappa.\end{split}

(3.1.9)

Proof.

The claim follows by combining Lemma
3.1.1
and formulas
(
3.0.7
) and (
3.1.6
).

∎

For future reference, we consider the integral curves of the
vector field
\vec{V}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}
associated to the background
circle front family

3.1.1
Flow curves of the vector fields
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}

Next, we consider the flow curves of the
vector field
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
in the space
time

\Omega_{large}\times\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\theta}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{y}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
Let

{\hat{t}}\in{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}
and

s\to\mu_{{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}}}(s)\in\Omega_{large}\times\mathbb{R}
be the curves satisfying

\partial_{s}\mu_{{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}}}(s)={-\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}}({\mu_{{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}}}(s)}),\qquad\mu_{{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}}}(0)=({\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}})

(3.1.10)

and let us write this curve in the coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as
\mu(s)=\mu_{{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta},{\hat{t}}}(s)=(\mu^{x}(s),\mu^{y}(s),\mu^{\theta}(s),\mu^{t}(s))
.

Below, we use the projection
\pi:\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\to\Omega
, given by
\pi(x,y,\theta,t)=(x,y,\theta)
, and
denote
the projections
of the paths by
\pi(\mu_{x,y,\theta}(s))
.
Note that
{\pi(\mu_{x,y,\theta}(s))}
is independent of the choice of
\hat{t}.

A Lemma that will prove useful, which crucially relies on the fact that
\kappa_{0}(x,y)\leq\kappa_{*}<0
in
\Omega_{\rm large}
is:

Lemma 3.1.2
.

Let
T>0
be chosen as
in (
2.2.8
) using
\epsilon_{0}
and
\delta_{0}
.
When
\epsilon_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
are small enough,
for

{(x,y,\theta)}\in\Omega_{small}{=\Omega(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1})}

the path
\mu(s)=\mu_{{x,y,\theta},{t}}(s)
intersects
\Gamma\times\mathbb{R}
at a value
{s=}\hat{s}({x,y,\theta}){\geq 0}

satisfying

\hat{s}{(x,y,\theta)}\leq{2\max(1,\|\alpha_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{small})})\cdot}(\epsilon_{1}+2\delta_{1}).

(3.1.11)

(Note that
\hat{s}{(x,y,\theta)}
is independent of
{t})
.
Moreover, choosing
\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}
further small relative to
T>0
,
the
t
coordinate of the path satisfies

|\mu^{t}(\hat{s}(x,y,\theta))-{\hat{t}}|<\frac{1}{4}T.

(3.1.12)

Proof.

We observe that since
\kappa<0
in
\Omega_{large}
,
\mu^{\theta}(s)
does not cross the value
\pi/2
, but gets closer to the value
\pi/2
when
s
grows. The derivatives
\partial_{s}\mu^{x}(s)
and
\partial_{s}\mu^{y}(s)
can
be considered as the velocity of the path
\mu(s)
in the
x
and
y
directions.
By using (
3.1.7
) and (
3.1.10
), we observe that
\partial_{s}\mu^{x}(s)
and
\partial_{s}\mu^{y}(s)
do not change their sign as
s
varies,
and
\max(|\partial_{s}\mu^{x}(s)|,|\partial_{s}\mu^{y}(s)|)\geq\frac{1}{2}\min(1,\|\alpha_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{small})}^{-1})
.
Thus, when
{(x,y,\theta)}\in\Omega_{small}
and
s
increases from
the value zero,
we see that the path
(\mu^{x}(s),\mu^{y}(s),\mu^{\theta}(s))
intersects
\Gamma
at a value of
s=\hat{s}({{\hat{x}},{\hat{y}},\hat{\theta}})
satisfying (
3.1.11
).
As
|\partial_{s}\mu^{t}(s)|\leq{1}
, this implies (
3.1.12
) when
\epsilon_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
are small enough.
∎

Armed with this key property, we introduce an
integral operator that inverts
\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}+P
.
Below, let
\vec{\nu}
denote the outward pointing unit normal vector of
\Omega_{small}
and let

\displaystyle\Gamma_{-}=\{(x,y,\theta)\in\Gamma:\ \langle\vec{\nu},\vec{V}_{0}\rangle<0\}.

(3.1.13)

Invoking Lemma
3.1.2
, we make the following definition.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
P(x,y,\theta,t)
be a continuous function on
\overline{\Omega}_{small}\times{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}
.
Then, for
any continuous function
f(x,y,\theta,t)
over
\overline{\Omega}_{small}\times{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}

we define for
{(x,y,\theta,t)\in}\overline{\Omega}_{small}\times{[-T,-\frac{3}{4}T]}

\begin{split}&{[}\mathcal{I}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0},P}{]}f(x,y,\theta,t)=[\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}+P]^{-1}f(x,y,\theta,t)\\
&=e^{-Q(x,y,\theta,t,\hat{s}(x,y,\theta))}\int_{0}^{\hat{s}(x,y,\theta)}e^{Q(x,y,\theta,t,s)}f(\mu_{x,y,\theta,t}(s))ds,\end{split}

(3.1.14)

where
Q(x,y,\theta,t,s)=\int_{0}^{s}P(\mu_{x,y,\theta,t}(\sigma))d\sigma.

In particular, below we will use that operator with
P=0
, that is,

\vec{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}f(x,y,\theta,t)=\int_{0}^{\hat{s}(x,y,\theta)}f(\mu_{x,y,\theta,t}(s))ds.

(3.1.15)

For notational simplicity, we denote

\vec{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}
by

\vec{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}_{0}
, for short.
We also consider the inverse of the differential operator
\vec{V}_{0}
,
see (
3.1.8
),

\vec{V}^{-1}_{0}h(x,y,\theta)=\int_{0}^{\hat{s}(x,y,\theta)}h({\pi(\mu_{x,y,\theta}(s))})ds,\quad h\in L^{2}(\Omega_{small}),

(3.1.16)

and the operator
(\vec{V}_{0}+P(x,y,\theta))^{-1}

defined analogously to the formula (
3.1.14
).

3.1.2
Commutation formulas for
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}

Since our argument will ultimately rely on energy estimates in high enough
Sobolev spaces, we will need to commute our equations with
\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{\theta}
; we put down commutation formulas of
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
with these vector fields:

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt[\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0},\partial_{x}]=\cos(\theta)\bigg(-\frac{\partial_{x}\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{0}^{2}}\partial_{y}+{}(\partial_{x}\kappa_{0})\partial_{\theta}\bigg),

(3.1.17)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt[\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0},\partial_{y}]=\cos(\theta)\bigg(-\frac{\partial_{y}\alpha_{0}}{\alpha_{0}^{2}}\partial_{y}+{}(\partial_{y}\kappa_{0})\partial_{\theta}\bigg),

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt[\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0},\partial_{\theta}]=-\cos(\theta)\partial_{x}+\sin(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha_{0}}}\partial_{y}+{}\sin(\theta)\kappa_{0}\partial_{\theta}.

For the rest of this paper we will be focused on proving the
rigidity Theorem
4
. In the next section we will
introduce a notion of
difference
of a circlefront and a pseudo-circlefront families and
their associated parameters, eventually showing that these differences
vanish, establishing Theorem
4
.

4
The difference of two pseudo-circlefront families, as a system of PDEs.

We will define a suitable notion of
difference
between
two pseudo-circle families; more precisely we only need to define this between a circle family and a pseudo-circle family. Since pseudo-circle families live over the non-linear circle bundle
S^{1}(M^{2}_{0},g_{0})
, the subtraction occurs on the level of the
parameter space

{\rm Param}
, by considering the differences

\Psi^{-1},\Psi_{0}^{-1}
over
S^{1}(M^{2}_{0},g_{0})
.

Lemma 4.0.1
.

Let
\vec{\Psi}_{0}:\tilde{\Omega}_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]\to S\widetilde{M}_{1}
be
a Circle front family and
\vec{\Psi}:\Omega_{large}\times[-2T,0]\to S\widetilde{M}_{1}
be a Pseudo-circle front family.
Recall
\Omega_{small}=(0,\epsilon_{1})\times(-\delta_{1},\delta_{1})\times(0,\pi)
.

(i) When
\epsilon_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
are small enough relative to
\Omega_{large}
,
there is a
{{\cal C}^{m-1}}
-smooth function
H:\Omega_{small}\to\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
,

\displaystyle H(x,y,\theta)=(\,x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta)\,)

such that

\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta),t)=\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t),

(4.0.1)

for all
(x,y,\theta,t)\in\Omega_{{small}}\times[-2T,0].

(ii) When
\vec{\Psi}
is a Pseudo-circle front family and (A1)-(A2) are satisfied, the map
F(x,y,\theta)
in (i) satisfies
F(x,y,\theta)=0
for

(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega_{large}^{known}
,
and particular for all
(x,y,\theta)\in\Gamma
.
Moreover
F(x,y,\pi/2)=0
, for all

(x,y)\in\Omega_{large}
.

Definition 4.0.1
.

Let
F(x,y,\theta)=(a(x,y,\theta),b(x,y,\theta),c(x,y,\theta))
, see (
4.0.1
).

We note that we defined the set
\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
in (
2.2.7
) to
make the definition of the above map
H
easier.

Proof.

(i)
We denote
\Psi^{t}(x,y,\theta)=\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
\Psi^{t}_{0}(x,y,\theta)=\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
.
Moreover,
assume that
\epsilon_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
defining the set
\Omega_{small}
are so small that

\Psi^{-T}(\Omega_{small})\subset V_{0}
, where

V_{0}=\Psi_{0}^{-T}(\tilde{\Omega}_{large})
.
Then we can define
a
C^{m-3}
-smooth map

H:\Omega_{small}\to\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
, or equivalently the functions
(a,b,c)
, by

H=(\Psi_{0}^{-T})^{-1}\circ\Psi^{-T}:\Omega_{small}\to\tilde{\Omega}_{large}.

(4.0.2)

Since both
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
and
\vec{\Psi}
are adapted
to the geodesic flow of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
, see (
2.2.20
),
this implies that (
4.0.1
) is valid for all
t\in[-2T,0]
.
Thus,
H
satisfies the claim (i).

(ii)
Since both
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
and
\vec{\Psi}
are adapted
to the geodesic flow of
(\widetilde{M},\widetilde{g})
,
this implies that (
4.0.1
) is valid when
T
is replace by any
t\in[-2T,0]
,
that is,

H=(\Psi_{0}^{-T})^{-1}\circ\Psi^{-T}=(\Psi_{0}^{-t})^{-1}\circ\Psi^{-t}.

(4.0.3)

Let
(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega_{large}^{known}
and recall that
\Gamma\subset\Omega_{large}^{known}
.
Then by (A1) and Remark
2.9
,

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,0)=\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,0)
.
Thus by (
4.0.3
) with
t=0
,
we have
F(x,y,\theta)=0
. The claim that
F(x,y,\pi/2)=0
follows from
condition (
2.3.14
).

∎

Definition 4.0.2
.

We use notations
\overline{\delta}\alpha(x,y),\delta\alpha(x,y)
and
\overline{\delta}\kappa(x,y),\delta\kappa(x,y)

where

\displaystyle{\overline{\delta}}\alpha(x,y,\theta)=\alpha(x,y)-\alpha_{0}(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta)),

(4.0.4)

\displaystyle\delta{\alpha}(x,y)=\alpha(x,y)-\alpha_{0}(x,y)

and the notations for
\delta\kappa
and
\delta\kappa
are similar.
Analogously, for the fields
J,\dot{J},Y,\dot{Y},{\bf B},\dot{\bf B}
we denote

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\overline{\delta}J(x,y,\theta,t)=J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-J_{0}^{\perp}(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta),t),

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt\delta J(x,y,\theta,t)=J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-J_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)

and similarly for
\overline{\delta}Y,\delta{Y},\overline{\delta}{\bf B},\delta{\bf B}
.

Definition 4.0.3
.

We denote by
F^{I}
,
Y^{I},

J^{I}
, and
{\bf B}^{I}
, where
I\in\mathbb{N}
, the
\partial_{\theta}
-derivative
of order
I
:

\displaystyle F^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}F,\ Y^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}Y^{\perp},\ J^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}J^{\perp},\ {\bf B}^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}{\bf B}^{\perp}.

\overline{\delta}Y^{I},\delta{Y}^{I},\overline{\delta}{\bf B}^{I},\delta{\bf B}^{I}
are the
\partial_{\theta}^{I}

derivative of the corresponding difference, e.g.
\overline{\delta}Y^{I}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{\theta}^{I}(\overline{\delta}Y(x,y,\theta,t)),

\delta Y^{I}(x,y,\theta,t)=\partial_{\theta}^{I}(\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t))
, etc. We also separately consider the lists of all derivatives up to order
I
, e.g.,

\displaystyle F^{[I]}=(F^{J})_{J\leq I}=(F,\partial_{\theta}F,\dots,\partial_{\theta}^{I}F).

The evolution equations (
3.0.1
) and (
3.0.2
), applied to both the Circle front family
\vec{\Psi}_{0}

and the
Pseudo-circle front family
\vec{\Psi}
imply the following system of first order equations on
F
, in the
x
- and
y
-directions.

Proposition 4.0.1
.

The map
F=(a,b,c)
satisfies

{\overline{A}}\hskip-2.84526pt\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\hskip-2.84526pt\partial_{x}a(x,y,\theta)\\
\hskip-2.84526pt\partial_{x}b(x,y,\theta)\\
\hskip-2.84526pt\partial_{x}c(x,y,\theta)\end{array}\right)\hskip-2.84526pt=\hskip-2.84526pt-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\hskip-2.84526pt{\cos(\theta)\overline{\delta}J(x,y,\theta)+\big(\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))-\cos(\theta)\big)\,J_{0}^{\perp}}\\
\hskip-2.84526pt{\cos(\theta)\overline{\delta}\dot{J}(x,y,\theta)+\big(\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))-\cos(\theta)\big)\,\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}}\end{array}\right)

(4.0.5)

and

{\overline{A}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{y}a(x,y,\theta)\\
\partial_{y}b(x,y,\theta)\\
\partial_{y}c(x,y,\theta)\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\cos(\theta)\overline{\delta}\alpha(x,y)+(\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))-\cos(\theta))\alpha_{0}}\\
\overline{\delta}{Y}(x,y,\theta)\\
\overline{\delta}\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta)\end{array}\right)

(4.0.6)

where

{\overline{A}}
is the matrix

\displaystyle{\overline{A}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sin(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))&\alpha_{0}\cdot\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))&0\\
-\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))J_{0}^{\perp}&Y_{0}^{\perp}&{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\\
-\cos(\theta+c(x,y,\theta))\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\end{array}\right),

where
\alpha_{0}
and the functions
J_{0}^{\perp}
,
Y_{0}^{\perp}
,
\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}
and their time derivatives
are evaluated at the point
(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta),{-T})
.

Proof.

We recall that
\Psi(x,y,\theta,t)
satisfy
the equations (
3.0.1
), (
3.0.2
), and (
3.0.3
)
and
that the similar equations hold for
\Psi_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})
when
J,Y,{\bf B},\alpha
are replaced
by
J_{0},Y_{0},{\bf B}_{0},\alpha_{0}
, respectively.

The proof below uses the fact that the chain rule applied to the derivative of the equation
(
4.0.1
)
implies

D\vec{\Psi}^{t}=D\vec{\Psi}_{0}^{t}\circ(Id+DF),

(4.0.11)

where
\vec{\Psi}^{t}(x,y,\theta)=\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t)
and
\vec{\Psi}_{0}^{t}(x,y,\theta)=\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
which we next consider in the local coordinates.

When we substitute in the first equation of (
3.0.1
) the formula

\Psi(x,y,

\theta,{-T})=\Psi_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})
, given by (
4.0.1
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}{\bigg|_{x=x_{*}}}(\Psi^{1}(x,y_{*},\theta_{*},{-T}))

(4.0.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\nabla_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T})\bigg){\,}(1+\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}a(x,y_{*},\theta_{*}))

\displaystyle+\bigg(\nabla_{y}|_{y=y_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x_{*}+a_{*},y+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T})\bigg){\,}(\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}b(x,y_{*},\theta_{*}))

\displaystyle+\bigg(\nabla_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta+c_{*},{-T})\bigg){\,}(\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}c(x,y_{*},\theta_{*})).

where
a_{*}=a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})
,
b_{*}=b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})
, and
c_{*}=c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})
.

The equation (
3.0.1
)
gives a formula for
\nabla_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T})

and similarly,
the equations (
3.0.2
) and (
3.0.3
),
evaluated at the point
(x+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T})

give formulas for
\nabla_{y}\Psi_{0}^{1}
and
\nabla_{\theta}\Psi_{0}^{1}
.

When we write the first equation of (
3.0.1
) for
\Psi_{0}

at the point
(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta+c_{*},{-T})
, and subtract from the obtained equation
the above equation (
4.0.12
),
the
R\vec{\Psi}_{0}
components of the obtained formula give us

\begin{split}&\quad-\cos(\theta_{*}+c_{*})\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T}){\,}(\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}a(x,y_{*},\theta_{*}))\\
&\quad+\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T}){\,}(\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}b(x,y_{*},\theta_{*}))\\
&\quad+\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},{-T}){\,}(\partial_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}c(x,y_{*},\theta_{*}))\\
&={-\cos(\theta_{*})\overline{\delta}J^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t_{*})}\\
&\quad{-(\cos(\theta_{*}+{c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})})-\cos(\theta_{*})){\,}\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a_{*},y_{*}+b_{*},\theta_{*}+c_{*},t_{*})\}}\end{split}

(4.0.13)

This gives the third row of the matrix equation (
4.0.5
).
By comparing the
\vec{\Psi}_{0}
components of the obtained formula
we obtain the first row of the matrix equation (
4.0.5
).

By doing the analogous analysis for the second equation in (
3.0.1
)
that we did above for the
first equation and comparing the
R\vec{\Psi}_{0}
components of the obtained equation we prove
the third row of the matrix equation (
4.0.5
).
Further, (
4.0.6
)
follows from similar computations using
the equation (
3.0.2
).

∎

Lemma 4.0.2
.

The matrix
{\overline{A}}
has the inverse

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt{\overline{A}}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sin({\theta+c})&{\dot{\bf B}_{0}}\cos({\theta+c})&-{{\bf B}_{0}}\cos({\theta+c})\\
\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\cos({\theta+c})&-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}{\dot{\bf B}_{0}}\sin({\theta+c})&\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}{{\bf B}_{0}}\sin({\theta+c})\\
{}\kappa_{0}\cos({\theta+c})&{\dot{J}_{0}}\cos^{2}({\theta+c})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}{\dot{Y}_{0}}\sin({\theta+c})&-{J_{0}}\cos^{2}({\theta+c})-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}{Y_{0}}\sin({\theta+c})\end{array}\right)

where
\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}
,
J_{0}^{\perp}
,
Y_{0}^{\perp}
,
{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}
and their time derivatives
are evaluated at the point
(x+a(x,y,\theta),y+b(x,y,\theta),\theta+c(x,y,\theta),{-T})
and

(a,b,c)=(a(x,y,\theta),b(x,y,\theta),c(x,y,\theta))
.

Proof.

Using (
2.3.2
)-(
2.3.4
) we see that

\displaystyle\hbox{det}\,({\overline{A}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbox{det}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sin(\theta+c)&\alpha_{0}\cdot\cos(\theta+c)&0\\
-\cos(\theta+{c{}{}})J_{0}^{\perp}&Y_{0}^{\perp}&{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\\
-\cos(\theta+{c{}{}})\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cos^{2}(\theta+c)}\alpha_{0}(\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}J_{0}^{\perp}-{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp})+\sin(\theta+c)(\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}Y_{0}^{\perp}-{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\alpha_{0}(x+a,y+b).

Thus,

using Cramer’s rule, we obtain the claim.
∎

Below, we denote

\displaystyle{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sin(\theta)&\alpha_{0}(x,y)\cdot\cos(\theta)&0\\
-\cos(\theta)J_{0}^{\perp}&Y_{0}^{\perp}&{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\\
-\cos(\theta)\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}&\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}\end{array}\right),

where functions
Y_{0}^{\perp}
etc. are evaluated at
(x,y,\theta,-T)
.
Then,
{\overline{A}}=A+{\mathcal{O}}(F)
, where
\mathcal{O}(F)
is an error term
of the form given in Definition
4.1.1
below.

Our next goal is to
find formulas for
\delta{\bf B},\delta\dot{\bf B}

in terms of
\partial_{\theta}F,F
. This will be a first step towards
writing the RHSs of equations (
4.0.5
), (
4.0.6
) purely in terms of the variables
\partial_{\theta}F,F,\delta Y^{\perp}
.

4.1
Expressions of
\delta{\bf B}
and
\delta\dot{{\bf B}}
in terms of

\partial_{\theta}F,F

Before we put down formulas for
\delta{\bf B},\delta\dot{\bf B}
it is useful to introduce two key notions of “correction terms” which will appear in many of the formulas we will derive in this paper.

Definition 4.1.1
.

We denote by

{{\cal O}}(A_{1},\dots,A_{j})
a general function of the input functions
A_{1},\dots,A_{j}
as well as of
x,y,\theta
(and possibly
t
),
for all
k\leq{\rm min}_{i}(r_{i})
:

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\bigg|{D^{k}_{x,y,\theta,t}}\bigg[{{\cal O}}(A_{1},\dots,A_{j}){(x,y,\theta,t)}\bigg]\bigg|\leq C_{0}{\sum_{l=0}^{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{j}|{D_{x,y,\theta,t}^{l}}\bigg[A_{i}(x,y,\theta,t)\bigg]|.

where
D_{x,y,\theta,t}
stands for the gradient in the parameters

x,y,\theta,t
, each
r_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
is the assumed regularity
{\cal C}^{r_{i}}
of the input function
A_{i}
,
and
C_{0}
depends only on a priori

{\cal C}^{m-1}
bounds of the maps

\Psi_{0},\Psi:{\rm Param}\to S^{1}M
.

10
10

10

Recall that
F(x,y,\theta)
has regularity
{\cal C}^{m-1}
in
x,y,\theta
,
\alpha(x,y),\kappa(x,y)
have regularity

{\cal C}^{m-2}
in
x,y
,
\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t),\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,t),\delta{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,t),\delta\dot{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,t)

have regularity
{\cal C}^{m-2}
in
x,y,\theta,t)
. Thus, for example, if
A_{1}=\partial_{y}\kappa
then
r_{1}=m-3
, for
A_{2}=\partial^{2}_{\theta}F
,
r_{2}=m-3
and for
A_{3}=\partial_{\theta}\delta\dot{\bf B}
.
r_{3}=m-3
).

We also adopt the following notation: If all input functions
A_{i}
listed do not depend on the parameter
t
(for example because they are
\partial_{y}\alpha(x,y)
which by construction is independent of
t
),
or
they correspond to a parameter evaluated at a
fixed
time, such as
\delta Y|_{t=-T}
, then writing
{\cal O}(A_{1},\dots,A_{j})
will mean that the function is independent of those parameter
t
. If the function
is allowed
to depend on
t
, we will write
{\cal O}_{t}(A_{1},\dots,A_{j})
,
to stress this dependence.

We start with some useful formulas:

Lemma 4.1.1
.

For
\delta{\bf B}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)={\bf B}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)-{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)
it holds that

\begin{split}&\delta{{\bf B}}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)\\
&=-\cos(\theta_{*})J_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta)\\
&\quad+Y_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))\\
&\quad+{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))+{\cal O}(F)\end{split}

(4.1.2)

where
{\cal O}(F)
is a
function
defined in Def.
4.1.1
.
An analogous formula for
\delta\dot{{\bf B}}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)

can be obtained by taking time derivative of (
4.1.2
),

\begin{split}&\delta\dot{{\bf B}}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)\\
&=-\cos(\theta_{*})\dot{J}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta)\\
&\quad+\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))\\
&\quad+\dot{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))+{\cal O}(F)\end{split}

(4.1.3)

Proof.

We recall that

\displaystyle{\bf B}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)=\langle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}\Psi^{1}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta,t),R\vec{\Psi}^{2}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)\rangle,

where

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}\Psi^{1}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}\bigg(\Psi_{0}^{1}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta),\theta+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta),t)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(\nabla_{x}|_{x=x_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t)\bigg){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))

\displaystyle+\bigg(\nabla_{y}|_{y=y_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t)\bigg){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))

\displaystyle+\bigg(\nabla_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}\Psi_{0}^{1}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t)\bigg){\,}(1+\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta)).

Hence, by (
3.0.1
)
and (
3.0.2
),

\begin{split}&{{\bf B}}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)\\
&={\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t)\\
&\quad+{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta))\\
&\quad-\cos(\theta_{*}+{c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})})J_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t){\,}\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta)\\
&\quad+{Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),y_{*}+b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),\theta_{*}+c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*}),t){\,}(\partial_{\theta}|_{\theta=\theta_{*}}b(x_{*},y_{*},\theta)).\end{split}

(4.1.4)

Moreover, using Taylor series, we see that

{{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*}+a,y_{*}+b,\theta_{*}+c,t)={{\bf B}}_{0}^{\perp}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)+{\cal O}(a,b,c)
. Similar formulas based on Taylor
approximations hold for
J_{0}^{\perp}
,
Y_{0}^{\perp}
and
\cos(\theta_{*}+{c(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*})})
.
Hence, we see

\displaystyle\overline{\delta}{{\bf B}}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)=\delta{{\bf B}}(x_{*},y_{*},\theta_{*},t)+{\cal O}(a,b,c).

These yield the equation (
4.1.2
); (
4.1.3
) follows similarly.

∎

In view of Proposition
4.0.1
and in view of Proposition

3.1.1
as well as the Lemma
4.1.1
,
our next goal is to derive formulas for the functions

\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y}
and
\delta J,\delta\dot{J}

in terms
of the function
F
and its
\partial_{\theta}
-derivatives.
The formulas will
also
involve the functions
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa,\partial_{y}\delta\alpha,\partial_{y}\delta\kappa
as source terms.

4.2
Equations for
{\delta}Y,{\delta\dot{Y}}
in
terms of
\partial_{\theta}F,F,\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
.

Next, we will write
\delta{Y}
,
\delta\dot{Y}
and
\delta J
,
\delta\dot{J}
appearing in the above equations
(
4.0.5
) and (
4.0.6
)
in terms of
\delta\alpha,

\delta\kappa
,

\delta\dot{\bf B}
and
\delta\dot{\bf B}
.

We consider the equation (
3.1.9
) and
the corresponding equation for
Y_{0}^{\perp}
and subtract them. To describe the resulting equation concisely we introduce some notation:

Definition 4.2.1
.

(Notations for generic correction terms)

We will denote by
{\cal K}
the pair of functions
(\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa)
; when we include superscripts
{}^{x},{}^{y},{}^{yy}
we mean all derivatives of the functions
(\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa)

up to
the listed orders. So
{\cal K}^{y}
will include all
y
-derivatives of
(\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa)
,
{\cal K}^{xy,yy}
will include all
\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{x}\partial_{y},\partial_{y}\partial_{y}
-derivatives of
(\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa)
etc.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

The scalar

\delta{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)={{Y}}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-{{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
satisfies at all points
(x,y,\theta,t)\in\Omega_{large}\times[{-2T},{0}]

\displaystyle(\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}+P_{\alpha_{0}})\delta{{Y}}(x,y,\theta,t)=\cos(\theta)\,S,

(4.2.1)

\displaystyle S=_{def}\partial_{y}\kappa_{0}{\,}\delta{{\bf B}}+{\mathcal{O}_{t}}({\cal K}^{y}),

Moreover,

\displaystyle\delta Y(x,y,\theta,t)=0,\quad\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)=0,

(4.2.2)

\hbox{when }(x,y,\theta)\in\Gamma,\ t\in{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}.

Remark 4.1
.

For future reference
we invoke Lemma
4.1.1
to also express the RHS of the above equation in terms of
\partial_{\theta}F=(\partial_{\theta}a,\partial_{\theta}b,\partial_{\theta}c)
instead of the terms
\delta{\bf B}^{\perp}
and
\delta\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}

\displaystyle S={\mathcal{O}_{t}({\partial_{\theta}}F,F,{\cal K}^{y})}

(4.2.3)

Proof.

Recall that above we have shown that
{{Y}}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
satisfies
the equation (
3.1.9
).
The same equation as (
3.1.9
) holds
for the field
{{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}={{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
with all
terms
Y,\dots,\kappa
replaced by
Y_{0},\dots,\kappa_{0}
.
When the equation (
3.1.9
) and the corresponding
equation for
{{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}
, both evaluated at the points
(x,y,\theta,t)
,
are subtracted, we obtain (
4.2.1
).

By using condition (A1) in Definition
2.2.3
, see also the formula (
2.3.21
) in Remark
2.9
, we obtain
that

\vec{\Psi}(x,y,\theta,t_{0})=S_{\widetilde{M},M}^{t_{0}}(\vec{\Phi}_{0}(x,y,\theta))=\vec{\Psi}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t_{0})

for all
(x,y,\theta)\in\Gamma
and
t_{0}\in{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}
. Hence, the boundary condition (
4.2.2
) is valid.
∎

Similarly, we can take the time derivative of the equation (
3.1.9
) and
apply the fact that
{{\bf B}}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
satisfies the Jacobi equation along
geodesic
t\to\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)
, that is,

\partial_{t}^{2}{\bf B}^{\perp}=-R_{M}{\bf B}^{\perp}
, where
R_{M}=R_{M}(\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t))
is the Gauss curvature
of
(M,g)
at the point
\Psi^{1}(x,y,\theta,t)
. By
subtracting the obtained equation and the corresponding equation for
Y_{0}^{\perp}
, we see that

\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)=\dot{Y}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)-\dot{Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)
,
satisfies an equation analogous to (
4.2.1
),

\displaystyle(\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}+P_{\alpha_{0}})\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,t)=\cos(\theta)\cdot{\mathcal{O}_{t}({\partial_{\theta}}F,F,{\cal K}^{y})}

(4.2.4)

Now, recalling the vanishing of
\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y}
on
\Gamma\times{[-T,-\frac{1}{2}T]}
and
Definition
3.1.2
and formula (
3.1.14
), we derive that:

\begin{split}&\delta{Y}(x,y,\theta,-T)\\
&=[\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}+P_{\alpha_{0}}]^{-1}\bigg[\cos(\theta)\cdot{\mathcal{O}_{t}({\partial_{\theta}}F,F,{\cal K}^{y})}\bigg](x,y,\theta,-T)\\
&\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,-T)\\
&=[\mathcal{V}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}+P_{\alpha_{0}}]^{-1}\bigg[\cos(\theta)\cdot{\mathcal{O}_{t}({\partial_{\theta}}F,F,{\cal K}^{y})}\bigg](x,y,\theta,-T)\end{split}

(4.2.5)

4.3
Equations for
\delta J
and
\delta\dot{J}
, in terms of

\delta{\bf B}^{\perp},\delta\dot{\bf B},\delta Y^{\perp},\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa,F
.

We now use equations (
4.2.5
) to derive equations for
\delta J^{\perp},\delta\dot{J}^{\perp}
over the domain
\Omega_{{large}}\times\{t=-T\}
, in terms of
\delta Y^{\perp}
and
\delta{\bf B}^{\perp}
as well as
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa,F
. We commence with some technical facts:

Consider the
Jacobi field

Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)={Y}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)+{}\alpha_{0}(x,y)\kappa_{0}(x,y){\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t).

(4.3.1)

Using the initial conditions for
{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}
and
{Y}_{0}^{\perp}

given in Lemma
2.2.1
,
we see that
for
\theta_{1}=0
or
\theta_{1}=\pi
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta_{1},t)|_{t=0}=0\quad\hbox{and}\quad\partial_{t}Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta_{1},t)|_{t=0}=0.

Since
t\to Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta_{1},t)
is a Jacobi field,
this implies that
Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta_{1},t)=0

for all
t
. In particular, the function

\displaystyle\rho_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)=\frac{Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,t)}{\sin(\theta)}

(4.3.2)

and its time derivative
\dot{\rho}_{0}(x,y,\theta,t)
are bounded near
\theta=0
and
\theta=\pi
.

Next, we consider the time
t=-T
.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\delta{J}(x,y,\theta,y,{-T})

(4.3.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\delta{Y}(x,y,\theta,{-T})+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\kappa_{0}(x,y)\delta{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,{-T})

\displaystyle+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})\delta\kappa(x,y)+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\mathcal{O}}(F,\delta\alpha).

By taking the time derivative of both sides, we obtain an analogous formula for

{\delta}\dot{J}(x,y,\theta,{-T})
, that is,

\displaystyle\delta\dot{J}(x,y,\theta,y,{-T})

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\delta\dot{Y}(x,y,\theta,{-T})+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\kappa_{0}(x,y)\delta\dot{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,{-T})

\displaystyle+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})\delta\kappa(x,y)+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\mathcal{O}}(F,\delta\alpha).

Proof.

Using Taylor series, we see that

{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})={\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})+{\mathcal{O}}(a,b,c)
and similar formulas
hold for
Y^{\perp}
and
J^{\perp}
.
Using these in formula (
3.1.6
), we see that

\displaystyle\overline{\delta}{J}(x,y,\theta,-T)=J^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,{-T})-J^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\overline{\delta}{Y}(x,y,\theta,{-T})+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\kappa_{0}(x,y)\overline{\delta}{\bf B}(x,y,\theta,{-T})

\displaystyle+{}\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})\overline{\delta}\kappa(x,y)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x+a,y+b)}{Y}^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})(\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta+c)})

\displaystyle{+{}\kappa_{0}(x+a,y+b){{\bf B}}^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})(\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta+c)})}

\displaystyle+{\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\mathcal{O}}(F,\delta\alpha).}

By using the Jacobi field
Z_{0}
, defined in (
4.3.1
)
and (
4.3.2
),
we see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x+a,y+b)}{Y}^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})(\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta+c)})

\displaystyle{+{}\kappa_{0}(x+a,y+b){{\bf B}}^{\perp}_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})(\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta+c)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}({\sin(\theta)}-{\sin(\theta+c)})\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x+a,y+b)}\frac{Z_{0}{(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})}}{{\sin(\theta+c)}}

Combining this with (
), formula
(
4.3.3
) for
\delta J
and the Taylor series approximations
\alpha_{0}(x+a,y+b)=\alpha_{0}(x,y)+{\mathcal{O}}(a,b)

and
Z_{0}(x+a,y+b,\theta+c,{-T})=Z_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})+{\mathcal{O}}(a,b,c)
,
and that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}({\sin(\theta)}-{\sin(\theta+c)})\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\frac{Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,-T)}{\sin(\theta)}=\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{\mathcal{O}}(F),

we obtain
the equation (
4.3.3
).

∎

Having obtained expressions for
\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y},\delta J,\delta\dot{J}
, we can now consider the evolution equations
(
3.0.1
),
(
3.0.2
) applied to

both
the families
\Psi_{0}
and
\Psi

and formally subtract them. We denote the resulting equations
by

\displaystyle{\rm DIFF}[\eqref{P partial x Gaussian restriction again}]{:=}\eqref{P partial x Gaussian restriction again}[\Psi]-\eqref{P partial x Gaussian restriction again}[\Psi_{0}],

\displaystyle{\rm DIFF}[\eqref{P partial_y decomposed again}]{:=}\eqref{P partial_y decomposed again}[\Psi]-\eqref{P partial_y decomposed again}[\Psi_{0}].

Our next goal is to translate these equations into a
system of evolution equations on
F(x,y,\theta)=\big(a,b,c\big)(x,y,\theta)
.

5
The difference of the two pseudo-circle families recast as a first order system in
F
, with extraneous source terms.

We are now ready to use Proposition
4.0.1
, and then Lemma
4.2.1
, (
4.2.4
) along with
Lemma
4.3.1

to recast the the evolution equations
{\rm DIFF}[\eqref{P partial x Gaussian restriction again}]
,

{\rm DIFF}[\eqref{P partial_y decomposed again}]
as a first order system in

F=(a,b,c)
. This system will include the functions
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
;
we see these are “source terms” for now.

5.1
The first order system and its
\partial_{\theta}
derivatives.

By
combining the formulas
(
4.0.5
), (
4.0.6
) and (
4.3.3
) with
Lemma
4.3.1
and the fact that the function
(
4.3.2
) is smooth up to and including
\theta=0,\pi
, we obtain

Lemma 5.1.1
.

For
(x,y,\theta)\in\tilde{\Omega}_{large}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\sin(\theta)A\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{x}a\\
\partial_{x}b\\
\partial_{x}c\end{array}\right)=-{\cos(\theta)}{\,}{L}\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{\theta}a\\
\partial_{\theta}b\\
\partial_{\theta}c\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle{+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
-{}\cos(\theta){\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})\delta\kappa(x,y)\\
-{}\cos(\theta)\dot{\bf B}^{\perp}_{0}(x,y,\theta,{-T})\delta\kappa(x,y)\end{array}\right)}-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\alpha_{0}}\delta Y|_{t=-T}\\
\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\alpha_{0}}\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt{+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
-\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\frac{Z_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,-T)}{\sin(\theta)}{\,}c\\
-\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\alpha_{0}(x,y)}\frac{\dot{Z}_{0}^{\perp}(x,y,\theta,-T)}{\sin(\theta)}c\end{array}\right)}{+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\cal O}_{t}({a,b,c})\\
{\cal O}_{t}({a,b,c},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha)\\
{\cal O}_{t}({a,b,c},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha)\end{array}\right)}

and

{A}{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{y}a\\
\partial_{y}b\\
\partial_{y}c\end{array}\right)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos(\theta)\delta\alpha\\
\delta{Y}|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)+{\cal O}{(a,b,c)},

(5.1.22)

where

\displaystyle{L}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&0\\
-{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)\cos(\theta)}J^{\perp}_{0}&{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)}{Y}_{0}^{\perp}&{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)}{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}\\
-{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)\cos(\theta)}\dot{J}^{\perp}_{0}&{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)}{\dot{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}&{}{\kappa_{0}(x,y)}\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}\end{array}\right),

and the functions
F=(a,b,c)
and
\delta{Y}^{\perp}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}
are evaluated at the point
(x,y,\theta)
and
the components of the vector fields
{Y}_{0}^{\perp}
,
{\dot{Y}}_{0}^{\perp}
,

{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}
,
\dot{\bf B}_{0}^{\perp}
and
\alpha_{0}
,
\kappa_{0}
are evaluated at the point

(x,y,\theta,-T)
.

We note that in the formula (
5.1.1
) the term containing
\frac{Z^{\perp}_{0}}{\sin(\theta)}c

can be absorbed to
\mathcal{O}_{t}(a,b,c)
, but we have not done this to emphasize that
c
is multiplied by factor

Z^{\perp}_{0}/\sin(\theta)
is not singular, but a smooth function.

We recall formulas (
4.2.1
), (
4.2.2
),
(
4.2.4
). These allow us to express

\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y}
(non-locally) in terms of
\partial_{\theta}F
. However, the
equations involve the functions
\delta\alpha(x,y),\delta\kappa(x,y)
. In Section
8

below we will be able to express these functions in terms of suitably high

\partial_{\theta}
derivatives of
F
at
\theta=\pi/2
. In order for us to see the system (
5.1.1
), (
5.1.22
) as a closed system, we are thus led to take
\partial_{\theta}
derivatives of (
4.2.4
). The derivation of the evolution equation on these
\partial_{\theta}
derivatives is our next goal.

Below, we introduce some further
short hand notation to to describe some terms
that in a suitable sense are “lower-order”.

Definition 5.1.1
.

We use the notation
{\cal D}{\mathcal{Y}}^{I}

for generic functions of
(x,y,\theta)

that satisfy
for
(x,y,\theta)\in\Omega_{large}
the pointwise estimate

\displaystyle|{\cal D}{\mathcal{Y}}^{I}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{J\leq{{I-1}}}(|\partial_{x}\delta{Y}^{J}|+|\partial_{y}\delta{Y}^{J}|+|\partial_{\theta}\delta{Y}^{J}|+|\delta{Y}^{J}|)\big|_{t=-T}

\displaystyle+C\sum_{J\leq{{I-1}}}(|\partial_{x}\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|+|\partial_{y}\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|+|\partial_{\theta}\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|+|\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|)\big|_{t=-T},

where we recall that
\delta{Y}^{J}=\partial_{\theta}^{J}\delta{Y}
,
\delta\dot{Y}^{J}=\partial_{\theta}^{J}\delta\dot{Y}
.

Differentiating formula (
5.1.1
) in
\theta

up to
I
times and using induction,
we obtain

\displaystyle\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}F^{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\cos(\theta)}A^{-1}{L}\partial_{\theta}F^{I}

(5.1.25)

\displaystyle+{{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{[I]}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{[I]}|_{t=-T})}

\displaystyle+{\sum_{J=0}^{I-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{J}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|_{t=-T})\bigg)}

\displaystyle{+\sum_{J=0}^{I}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J+1}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}(F^{[J]},\delta\kappa,\delta\alpha)\bigg).}

When we operate with the derivatives
\partial_{\theta}^{I}
to the both sides of the equation (
5.1.22
),
we obtain

{A}\partial_{y}F^{I}+\sum_{J\leq I-1}\binom{I}{J}(\partial_{\theta}^{I-J}A)\partial_{y}F^{J}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(\partial_{\theta}^{I}\cos(\theta))\delta\alpha(x,y)\\
\delta{Y}^{I}{|_{t=-T}}\\
\delta\dot{Y}^{I}{|_{t=-T}}\end{array}\right)+{{\cal O}(F^{[I]})},

(5.1.26)

where we recall that we denote
\delta Y^{{[}I]}=(\partial_{\theta}^{J}\delta Y)_{J\leq I},

F^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}F,
and
F^{[I]}=(\partial_{\theta}^{J}F)_{J\leq I}.

By applying induction in (
5.1.26
), we obtain

\partial_{y}F^{I}=A^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(\partial_{\theta}^{I}\cos(\theta))\delta\alpha\\
\delta{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}(F^{[I]},\delta{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\alpha)

(5.1.27)

We note that the right hand sides of the equations (
5.1.1
), (
5.1.22
) as well as their
\theta
-differentiated versions (
5.1.25
) and (
5.1.27
) do not appear to form a closed system of equations in
F
(or
F^{I}
). The reason is the appearance of extra functions
\delta Y^{I}|_{t=-T}
,
\delta\dot{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}
as well as
{\cal K}=(\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa)
in the right hand side.

A central challenge of our work is to express these “extraneous” parameters as functionals of
F^{I}
; in fact we will derive bulk bounds for them in terms of
F^{I},

I\leq{2}
.
But prior to this we seek to derive a “scalar” second order equation for
F^{I}
which (up to the “extraneous terms” we will deal with in the future) depends only on
\vec{V}_{0}
and
\partial_{y}
-derivatives of
F^{I}
.
In particular the highest order term is
\vec{V}_{0}\partial_{y}F^{I}
. Since the two vector fields
\vec{V}_{0},\partial_{y}
live over the 3-dimensional space
D_{\lambda}(z_{*})
, we will be calling the resulting equation a “semi-wave equation”.

5.2
The 1st order system re-cast as a semi-wave equation.

In this section it is important to recall that
F=F(x,y,\theta)
takes values in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
; in particular it can be thought of as a function taking values in the space of
3\times 1
matrices, which are acted on by
3\times 3
matrices
B
by multiplication
B\cdot F
. T0 stress this,
we write
{\overline{F}}
instead fo
F
for the remainder of this section only. We will revert to writing
F
after this section, for notational simplicity.

The semi-wave equation arises by taking a
{\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}}
derivative of the equation
(
5.1.26
) and using the fact that
{\overline{F}}
is independent of the
t
variable.
By applying (
5.1.27
), the resulting equation becomes:

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle A\vec{V}_{0}\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}=\vec{V}_{0}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(\partial_{\theta}^{I}\cos(\theta))\delta\alpha\\
\delta{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)+{\mathcal{O}(\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{[I]},{\overline{F}}^{[I]})}

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{O}(\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}\delta{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}\delta\dot{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\alpha)}.

We use below an improved equation when
I=0
: By taking the
\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{{0}}
derivative of
(
5.1.22
),
and using (
4.2.1
) and (
4.2.4
), we obtain

(5.2.6)

\displaystyle(\vec{V}_{0}+P_{\alpha_{0}})\partial_{y}{{\overline{F}}}={\mathcal{O}}({\overline{F}},\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}})+{\mathcal{O}}(\delta{Y}^{\perp}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{\perp}|_{t=-T},\cos(\theta){{\cal K}^{y}}).

Our next goal is to replace the “top order” term

\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}
in the RHS of the above by different “top order”
derivatives that will prove
more favorable to our analysis. This will not be very straightforward, in view of some non-invertibility of a matrix
{\cal M}_{0}
that appears naturally when we seek to achieve this by combining
the equations we have. The issue will be overcome by a more simplified equation that we will obtain for the part of
{\overline{F}}
that lies in the zero-eigenspace of
{\cal M}_{0}
.

5.2.1
Re-expressing some lower-order derivatives in terms of better derivatives.

We seek to obtain a formula for

{\cos(\theta)}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}
in terms of the more favorable derivatives
\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I},\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}
.

By definition of
\vec{V}_{0}
, see
Definition
3.1.1
,
we see that

\displaystyle-{}\cos(\theta){\,}\kappa_{0}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}=-\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I}+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha_{0}}}\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}+\sin(\theta)\partial_{x}{\overline{F}}^{I}.

(5.2.7)

By combining (
5.1.27
), (
5.1.25
)
and (
5.2.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg(A^{-1}{L}-{}\kappa_{0}\bigg){\cos(\theta)}{\,}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}

(5.2.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bigg(-\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I}+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha_{0}}}\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}\bigg)

\displaystyle+{\sum_{J=0}^{I}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{J}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|_{t=-T})\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{J=0}^{I-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}({\overline{F}}^{[J+1]},\delta\kappa,\delta\alpha)\bigg)

It will prove useful to consider the eigenvectors of the matrix

A^{-1}{L}-\kappa_{0}\,I
which appears in the first line of (
5.2.8
). We will show in the next Lemma that its eigenvectors are

\displaystyle{\overline{\omega}}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\theta)\\
\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\cos(\theta)\\
-\kappa_{0}\cos(\theta)\\
\end{array}\right),\quad{\overline{\omega}}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\cos(\theta)\\
\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\sin(\theta)\\
0\\
\end{array}\right),\quad{\overline{\omega}}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
1\\
\end{array}\right).

Lemma 5.2.1
.

The matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}{(x,y,\theta)}=-A^{-1}{(x,y,\theta)}{L}{(x,y,\theta)}+\kappa_{0}{(x,y)}\,I

satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}{\overline{\omega}}_{1}=\kappa_{0}{\overline{\omega}}_{1},\quad\mathcal{M}_{0}{\overline{\omega}}_{2}=0,\quad\mathcal{M}_{0}{\overline{\omega}}_{3}=0.

(5.2.10)

Proof.

Recall
that we denote the symplectic products by

\{(J,\dot{J}),(Y,\dot{Y})\}{(x,y,\theta)}=\dot{J}{(x,y,\theta)}Y{(x,y,\theta)}-J{(x,y,\theta)}\dot{Y}{(x,y,\theta)}.

(Below we suppress the explicit appearance of the variables
(x,y,\theta)
, for notational simplicity).

We see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}A^{-1}{L}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}-\cos^{2}(\theta)\{({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}}),(J_{0},\dot{J}_{0})\}&\cos(\theta)\{({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}}),(Y_{0},\dot{Y}_{0})\}&0\\
\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\sin(2\theta)\{({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}}),(J_{0},\dot{J}_{0})\}&-\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\sin(\theta{})\{({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}}),(Y_{0},\dot{Y}_{0})\}&0\\
-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\sin(2\theta)\{(Y_{0},\dot{Y}_{0}),(J_{0},\dot{J}_{0})\}&\cos^{2}(\theta)\{(J_{0},\dot{J}_{0}),(Y_{0},\dot{Y}_{0})\}&n_{33}\\
\end{array}\right),

where
n_{33}=\{(J_{0},\dot{J}_{0}),({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}})\}\cos^{2}(\theta)+\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}\{(Y_{0},\dot{Y}_{0}),({\bf B}_{0},\dot{{\bf B}_{0}})\}\sin(\theta).

Using the symplectic equations (
2.3.10
)-(
2.3.12
), that are valid also for a Circle front family,
we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathcal{N}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\cos^{2}\theta&-\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{0}\sin(2\theta{})&0\\
-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\sin(2\theta)&\sin^{2}\theta&0\\
-{}{{\frac{1}{2}}}\kappa_{0}\sin(2\theta)&-{}\kappa_{0}\cdot\alpha_{0}\cos^{2}(\theta)&1\\
\end{array}\right).

The eigenvalues of
\mathcal{N}
are
1
and the solutions of the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hbox{det}\,\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos^{2}\theta-\lambda&-\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{0}\sin(2\theta{})\\
-\frac{1}{2\alpha_{0}}\sin(2\theta)&\sin^{2}\theta{}-\lambda\end{array}\right)=\lambda(\lambda-1).

Hence, the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{N}
are
\{1,1,0\}
. By direct computation, we can verify that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{N}-I){\overline{\omega}}_{1}=-{\overline{\omega}}_{1},\quad(\mathcal{N}-I){\overline{\omega}}_{2}=0,\quad(\mathcal{N}-I){\overline{\omega}}_{3}=0.

(5.2.14)

This and the definition of matrices
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{M}
yield (
5.2.10
).
∎

It is also useful to put down the dual vectors of the vectors
\overline{\omega}_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle{\overline{\eta}}_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\sin(\theta)\\
-\alpha_{0}\cos(\theta)\\
0\\
\end{array}\right),\quad{\overline{\eta}}_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\cos(\theta)\\
\alpha_{0}\sin(\theta)\\
0\\
\end{array}\right),\quad\vec{\eta}_{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\kappa_{0}\sin(\theta)\cos(\theta)\\
-\alpha_{0}\kappa_{0}\cos^{2}(\theta)\\
1\\
\end{array}\right).

The latter satisfy
\langle\eta_{\alpha},\omega_{\beta}\rangle=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
. We also denote

P_{\alpha}v=\langle v,{\overline{\eta}}_{\alpha}\rangle\,{\overline{\omega}}_{\alpha}
.

Definition 5.2.1
.

We express
\overline{F}
in terms of the basis
\overline{\omega}_{j},j\in\{1,2,3\}

{\overline{F}}(x,y,\theta)=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\overline{F}_{(\alpha)}(x,y,\theta),\quad{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}=P_{\alpha}{\overline{F}}

(5.2.16)

and let
F_{(\alpha)}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}=F_{(\alpha)}\overline{\omega}_{\alpha}.

Below, we use that

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}(\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I})+\mathcal{O}({\overline{F}}^{[I]}).

(5.2.17)

The above formula can be used to return to (
5.2.8
) and obtain separate equations for
\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(\alpha)}
,
\alpha=1,2,3
. This is what leads to a key dichotomy between

{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(1)}
and
{\overline{F}}_{(\beta)}^{I}
,
\beta=2,3
. As we will see, for
\alpha=1
, (
5.2.8
) implies the solvability of
\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(1)}^{I}
in terms of
\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I}
,
\cos(\theta)\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}
,
plus
and lower-order terms (in a suitable sense). This, however is
not
true of
{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(\beta)}
,
\beta=2,3
. We
will
be able to obtain control of
\cos(\theta)\partial_{\theta}F^{I}_{(\beta)},\beta=2,3
because of some remarkable cancellation that will occur in the equation
for
\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(\beta)}
,
\beta=2,3
. We first derive two useful equations:

Below,
\delta_{\alpha,1}
satisfies
\delta_{\alpha,1}=1
if
\alpha=1
and
{\delta_{\alpha,1}}=0
if
\alpha=2,3
.

As
A^{-1}{L}
and
P_{\alpha}
commute, formulas
(
5.1.25
) and (
5.2.17
) and Lemma
5.2.1

imply for all
\alpha=1,2,3

\displaystyle\partial_{x}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}^{I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha,1}\cdot\kappa_{0}\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\theta)}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}^{I}

(5.2.18)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}{{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{[I]}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{[I]}|_{t=-T})}

\displaystyle+{\sum_{J=0}^{I-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J+1}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{J}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|_{t=-T})\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{J=0}^{I}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J+1}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}({\overline{F}}^{[J]},\delta\kappa,\delta\alpha)\bigg).

and (
5.1.27
) and (
5.2.17
)
imply for all
\alpha=1,2,3

\partial_{y}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(\alpha)}=P_{\alpha}A^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\delta{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}^{I}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)+\mathcal{O}({\overline{F}}^{[I]},\delta{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{[I-1]}|_{t=-T},\delta\alpha)

(5.2.19)

5.2.2
A remarkable cancellation in the transport equation
\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}_{(j)},j=2,3
.

By operating with the projection

P_{\alpha}
to the both sides of the equation (
5.2.7
) with
I=1
,
substituting
(
5.2.18
) and
(
5.2.19
) in the obtained equation,
with
I=1
and invoking (
5.2.17
), we obtain

(5.2.20)

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91692pt-\cos(\theta){\,}\kappa_{0}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{{(\alpha)}}=-\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}_{{(\alpha)}}-{\cos(\theta)}{\,}A^{-1}{L}\cdot\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle+\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{{\alpha_{0}}}A^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\delta{Y}|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)-\cos(\theta)\frac{1}{\alpha_{0}}A^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\delta Y|_{t=-T}\\
\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle+{\cal O}({\overline{F}},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha,\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\kappa)

(Observe that above the terms containing
\delta Y|_{t=-T}
and
\delta\dot{Y}|_{t=-T}
cancel each other) and thus

\displaystyle-\cos(\theta){\,}(A^{-1}{L}-\kappa_{0})\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{{(\alpha)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}_{{(\alpha)}}+{\cal O}({\overline{F}},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha,\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\kappa).

Formula (
5.2.17
) implies for
\alpha=2,3
,

(A^{-1}{L}-\kappa_{0})\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}=O({\overline{F}})
,
and for
\alpha=1
,

(A^{-1}{L}-\kappa_{0})\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}=-\kappa_{0}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}+O({\overline{F}})
.
Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}={\cal O}({\overline{F}},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha,\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\kappa),\quad\hbox{for $\alpha=2,3$},

(5.2.29)

(5.2.30)

\displaystyle\cos(\theta)\kappa_{0}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}=\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}+{\cal O}({\overline{F}},\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\alpha,\cos(\theta)\cdot\delta\kappa),\quad\hbox{for $\alpha=1$}

Our next step is to take
\partial_{\theta}
derives of the above two equations
(
5.2.29
) and (
5.2.30
), to derive analogues for
{\overline{F}}^{K}_{(\alpha)}
.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

For
\alpha\in\{{{2}},{3}\}
and
K\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{K}_{(\alpha)}-K\cdot\frac{\cos^{2}(\theta)}{\sin(\theta)}\cdot\kappa_{0}{\overline{F}}^{K}_{(\alpha)}+m_{\alpha,{2}}{\overline{F}}^{K}_{({2})}+m_{\alpha,{3}}{\overline{F}}^{K}_{({3})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal O}({\overline{F}}^{K}_{({1})})+\sum_{J=0}^{K-1}{\cal O}(\frac{1}{\sin^{K-J+1}(\theta)}{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}^{J})+\sum_{J=0}^{K-1}{\cal O}({\overline{F}}^{J})

\displaystyle+\sum_{J=1}^{K}{\cal O}(\frac{1}{\sin^{K-J+1}(\theta)}\delta{\widetilde{Y}}^{J-1},\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa),

where
m_{\alpha,{2}},m_{\alpha,{3}}
depend on
{\overline{F}}^{[K]}
and
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
.

For
\alpha=1
and
I\geq 1
we have:

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}\cdot{\cos(\theta)}{\,}\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(1)}

(5.2.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\vec{V}_{0}{\overline{F}}^{I}_{(1)}+{\sum_{J=0}^{I}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}(\delta Y^{J}|_{t=-T},\delta\dot{Y}^{J}|_{t=-T})\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{J=0}^{I-1}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sin^{I-J}(\theta)}{\cal O}_{t}({\overline{F}}^{[J+1]},\delta\kappa,\delta\alpha)\bigg)

Proof.

The claim follows by
computing the
\partial_{\theta}^{I}
derivatives of the formulas (
5.2.29
) and (
5.2.30
) and
using the
commutator formula (
3.1.17
)

together with (
5.2.18
), and (
5.2.19
).

∎

The above expressions for
{\overline{F}}_{(\alpha)}^{I}
will be used to obtain a bound (in a suitable
L^{2}
-space) on the derivatives

\cos(\theta)\cdot\partial_{\theta}{\overline{F}}^{I}
in terms of
\delta Y^{J},(J\leq I-1)
,
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
, and lower-order derivatives of
F
in section
7.2
below.

6
Differentiated equations for
\delta Y
, and a useful foliation of the bulk.

In this section we show Proposition
6.3.1
below.
Our starting point will be the transport equation
(
4.2.1
). It will be necessary to derive the
transport equation for the derivatives of
\delta Y
, so we commence with that derivation.

6.1
A transport equation on
\nabla_{x,y,\theta}\delta Y
and
\nabla_{x,y,\theta}\delta\dot{Y}
.

The evolution equation (
4.2.1
),
and its integral solution (
4.2.5
)
allows us to think of

\delta Y
,
\delta\dot{Y}
in terms of a suitable path
integral of
\partial_{\theta}F
,
\delta\alpha,\delta\kappa
.

We will be deriving equations for the parameters
\delta Y^{I}=\partial^{I}_{\theta}\delta Y,\delta\dot{Y}^{I}=\partial_{\theta}^{I}\delta\dot{Y}
. These will be derived from the transport equation (
4.2.1
), by taking
\partial_{\theta}
derivatives. However as we will see,
{\cal\vec{V}}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}
does
not
commute with
\partial_{\theta}
;
to deal with the corresponding commutation terms that are then introduced, we
need to obtain bounds for
\partial_{x}\delta Y,\partial_{y}\delta Y
. These will follow from the system of equations we derive in the next Lemma:

Lemma 6.1.1
.

The derivatives of
\delta Y
and
\delta\dot{Y}

satisfy the system of equations (here and below,
m
stands for a generic continuous function)

(6.1.1)

\displaystyle{{{\mathcal{D}}_{\alpha_{0},\kappa_{0}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{x}\delta Y\\
\partial_{x}\delta\dot{Y}\\
\partial_{y}\delta Y\\
\partial_{y}\delta\dot{Y}\\
\partial_{\theta}\delta Y\\
\partial_{\theta}\delta\dot{Y}\end{array}\right)=_{def}}(\vec{\mathcal{V}}_{0}-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta)}\mathcal{N}_{1}+\mathcal{N}_{2})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\partial_{x}\delta Y\\
\partial_{x}\delta\dot{Y}\\
\partial_{y}\delta Y\\
\partial_{y}\delta\dot{Y}\\
\partial_{\theta}\delta Y\\
\partial_{\theta}\delta\dot{Y}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt={\left(\begin{array}[]{c}{\frac{m\,{\cos(\theta)}}{\sin^{2}(\theta)}\delta Y+}m\cdot\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\theta)}\partial_{\theta}^{2}F+{\mathcal{H}^{xy}}\\
{\frac{m\,{\cos(\theta)}}{\sin^{2}(\theta)}\delta\dot{Y}+}m\cdot\frac{\cos(\theta)}{\sin(\theta)}\partial_{\theta}^{2}F+{\mathcal{H}^{xy}}\\
